normal K\"ahler category is not its stated theorem.

Several printed bridges in that first version need further
arguments. On physical page~9, generation with real
coefficients fails already for a section \(\rmi s_0\)
in a one-element complex basis; the intended Gram estimate
uses complex coefficients. The smooth-fibre proof gives
an intrinsic distance estimate from its tangential gradient
bound; the advertised uniform ambient H\"older bound
needs a uniform intrinsic--extrinsic bridge.

The path lemma on physical page~21 removes upstairs
loops, but does not control backtracking after projection.
On the normal threefold
\(uv=w^2\) times a line with coordinate \(b\), the
resolution map is
\[
 \pi(t,z,b)=(t,tz^2,tz,b).
\]
The injective exceptional arc
\(\eta(s)=(0,s,5s-12s^2+8s^3)\), \(0\leq s\leq1\),
projects to a backtracking path on the straight singular
segment \(0\leq b\leq1\). Its projected length is
\(1+2\sqrt6/9>1\), while the original straight segment
has length one. The arc has no upstairs loop for the
proposed removal procedure. This shows why that procedure
does not prove its asserted projected-length bound;
it does not exclude another well-chosen lift.
Transferring the resulting regular-locus paths to moving
fibres also needs control of prescribed endpoints and
of the error of a semipositive pulled-back metric.

The projective-matrix argument on physical page~27 needs
an additional bridge as well. On the smooth conic
\(Z=\{z_0z_1=z_2^2\}\subset\mathbb P^2\), set
\(A_a=\operatorname{diag}(a,a,a^{-2})\), \(a\geq1\).
This matrix has determinant one and unbounded norm.
With \(S=|z_0|^2+|z_1|^2\), \(T=|z_2|^2\leq S/2\),
its sup-normalized relative Fubini--Study potential is
\[
 u_a=\log\frac{S+a^{-6}T}{S+T},\qquad
 \log(2/3)\leq u_a\leq0.
\]
In the parametrization \([1:\zeta^2:\zeta]\), the
new potential is \(\log(1+|\zeta|^4+a^{-6}|\zeta|^2)\).
The metric ratio at \(\zeta=0\) is \(a^{-6}\), tending
to zero. Thus bounded normalized potentials on a
nondegenerate smooth subvariety do not alone bound the
special-linear matrix or give tensor equivalence.
Possible extra Bergman Gram constraints require a separate
argument. This example does not disprove a theorem with
such additional constraints verified.

These identified gaps keep the broader analytic and uniform
family claims unverified in this checkpoint. They are not
recorded as counterexamples to the main H\"older theorems.

\subsection{Smooth-base stability and a direction test}

\cite{GSS26GeometricStabilityV1} proves its distance
comparison on a fixed smooth compact base in a common
K\"ahler class. Its trace-deviation integral needs the
factor \(\sqrt V\) in
\eqref{eq:appH-ricci-trace-deviation} unless mass is
normalized to one. The one-sided segment argument uses
only the reference Ricci lower bound; the two-sided
identity-marked conclusion uses it for both metrics.
No converse moduli assertion modulo automorphisms is added.
The paper's rough-current construction has common density,
curvature, class and strong volume-convergence conditions.

For the cone in \eqref{eq:appH-ricci-cone-density}, write
\(T=r^2/(1+r^2)^2\) and choose \(a=1/2\). Its density
is the inverse of
\(2\sqrt T(1+2\sqrt T)\). Exact smooth approximations
are
\[
 f_\delta=\log\bigl(2\sqrt{T+\delta}
                         (1+2\sqrt{T+\delta})\bigr),
 \qquad
 \omega_\delta=c_\delta e^{-f_\delta}\theta,
\]
with \(c_\delta\) chosen to keep mass \(2\pi\).
For \(0<\delta\leq1/4\),
\(1\leq c_\delta\leq2+\sqrt2\) and
\(c_\delta\to1\). Since
\(\mathsf D\log(T+\delta)\geq-2\theta\) and
the corresponding scalar function has derivative between
\(1/2\) and one and nonnegative second derivative,
\(\mathsf Df_\delta\geq-2\theta\).
Thus \(\operatorname{Ric}(\omega_\delta)\geq0\),
the densities have a common \(L^p\) bound for \(1<p<2\),
and the metrics converge locally smoothly off the poles.
Uniform cap-diameter estimates and the radial formula give
the actual completed-distance limit; taking a product gives
the example in complex dimension two.

The forward Lipschitz identity asserted in Theorem~1.2
of both the Ricci-entropy and the geometric-stability
papers is false for this example. The quotient of the
cone distance by the background distance tends to infinity
at a pole. The proof's equation~(6.13) in the stability
paper has the correct direction:
\(\sqrt c\,d_\theta\leq d_\omega\leq C d_\theta^\beta\).
The completion-to-background identity is Lipschitz, and
the reverse identity has the H\"older bound. This correction
does not refute the canonical RCD conclusion or the
stability mechanism.

The positive-time smooth-base flow argument gives one-sided
noninflation. Its density estimate supplies strong volume
convergence, and the class must be repaired by adding
\(t\operatorname{Ric}(\theta)\) before applying the
fixed-class comparison. It does not yield two-sided flow
continuity or a flow theorem on an arbitrary singular base.

All formulas, imports and examples here are explicitly
attributed or direct convention calculations. No original
general theorem, independent model receipt or priority
claim is added. The forty-two canonical atoms and twelve
formal problem environments keep their existing statuses.
The general non-Archimedean envelope questions and BJ22's
critical homogeneous Siu-division step remain open; the
LPWZ density candidate remains unverified.

\section{Harmonic quiver norms and split attainment}
\label{sec:appL-harmonic-quiver}
\index{split norm!attainment of a minimum}
\index{CAT(0) space!norm potential}

The finite-dimensional comparison in
\cref{subsec:ch50-harmonic-quiver} uses the complete-field extension of
HKKP's harmonic-norm theory proved in \cite{BB26HarmonicQuiverV1}.
It is an attributed comparison and a collection of direct worked
calculations. It does not identify quiver stability with K-stability
of a polarized variety, or produce a Monge--Amp\`ere equation on
an analytification.

\subsection{The complete-field statement}

\cite[Theorem~6.23 and Question~6.24]{HKKP26CategoricalV1} gives the
polystability--harmonicity--semistability implications under spherical
completeness and asks whether that assumption can be omitted.
\cite[Theorem~1.1]{BB26HarmonicQuiverV1} supplies the complete-field
answer for finite quivers and finite-dimensional representations.
Its norms have arbitrary positive real weights. Real rescaling of
a norm is not the same as multiplication by an element of $K$;
no assumption that all relevant labels lie in the value group is used.
Loops and multiple arrows remain allowed, and no characteristic or
algebraic-closedness restriction is introduced.

There are three statements to keep separate. Theorem~3.10 gives
existence of an all-norm minimum for polystable degree-zero $E$.
Corollary~4.2 characterizes split minima by HKKP harmonicity.
Theorem~5.4 replaces any degree-zero all-norm minimum by a split
minimum with the same full arrow profiles. The third statement
uses exact feasible comparison; it does not follow from density
of split norms alone.

The degree in harmonicity is $\theta^\star$ on a filtered graded
object, as in \eqref{eq:ch50-quiver-harmonic-degree}. It has the
nonnegative-subobject convention. The auxiliary reduction in
Ben-Bassat's Section~4.2 instead uses a weight $\kappa$ with the
nonpositive-subobject convention on its feasible graded quiver.
These signs belong to different steps and are not interchangeable.
Both arrows of the main implication are strict: HKKP Example~6.21
has a semistable identity representation without a harmonic norm,
while Example~6.22 is semistable and nonpolystable with a harmonic
norm. The latter is extended to a family in Ben-Bassat Section~6.1.

\subsection{The metric, incidence and extension inputs}

The norm foundations are the published edition of \cite{BE21}:
finite-dimensional norm equivalence, exact split restriction and
quotient, density, common orthogonal bases, determinant products
and base change. Its printed Lemma~3.4 requires an explanation
at the sorting step. A determinant of a fixed flag prefix does
not equal the sum of the largest projected coordinate ratios.
For example, with $p(e_1)=p(e_2)=1$ and
$q(e_1)=e^{-1}$, $q(e_2)=e$, the first prefix ratio is $-1$,
whereas the largest ratio is $1$.

The needed sorted inequality follows by a finite triangular-minor
argument. Fix the full flag of a basis $(e_i)$. For split $p,q$,
choose orthogonal flag-adapted bases $(f_i)$ and $(g_i)$ with
$f_i,g_i\equiv e_i$ modulo the preceding flag step. Their weights
$a_i=p(f_i)$, $b_i=q(g_i)$ are the Gram--Schmidt quotient weights.
The matrix of the identity from $(f_i)$ to $(g_i)$ is upper
triangular with diagonal one. Every principal minor indexed by
an arbitrary subset $I$ is one. Therefore
\[
 \log\|\textstyle\bigwedge^j\operatorname{id}\|_{p\to q}
       \geq\sum_{i\in I}\log(b_i/a_i),\qquad |I|=j.
\]
Maximizing over all $I$ gives the inequality for the $j$ largest
projected ratios, with equality for $j=\dim V$ by the determinant
identity. This is majorization of the projected list by the full
relative spectrum. The symmetric convex-hull criterion then
gives contraction for every symmetric norm. The printed direction
and summation index in BE Lemma~3.6 must be read with that direction.
This explanation uses established split restriction--quotient and
exterior norm identities; it is not a new theorem or priority claim.

The CAT(0) and bounded convex intersection inputs are
\cite[Propositions~2.10 and 2.12]{Parreau00AffineAuthor}, checked in
the author's 41-page version at physical pages 19--20.
Its Proposition~3.12 at physical pages 35--36 gives the metric
comparison. The cited locators refer to that author file, not
to a presumed translation of published pages 263--302.
The split norm building can be incomplete. It is its completion
by all norms, followed by the single common-scaling slice, that
is used for minimization.

Some auxiliary wording in the author file needs correction.
In the determinant-absolute-value-one group, a diagonal matrix fixes
an apartment pointwise only when its diagonal entries have absolute
value one; arbitrary diagonal
matrices can translate it. Lemma~3.9's positive-log line must
be reconciled with its earlier negative-log apartment coordinates.
Its weighted germ-entry conditions describe a subgroup after
the determinant-absolute-value-one condition is imposed.
The elimination in Proposition~3.10 actually uses unipotent
operations, which satisfy that condition. These qualifications
do not refute the CAT(0) input or the constructed building.

The uniform incidence estimate uses
\cite[Theorem~1.3 and \S4.3.1]{MoretBailly12ClosedImageV3}.
Complete real-valued $K$ is Henselian and $\widehat K/K$ is the
identity extension, so its separability hypothesis holds.
The relevant Grassmannian incidence schemes are projective.
Closedness of their rational-point image supplies an excluded
neighborhood of the fixed representation and hence a uniform
positive coefficient gap over the finite forbidden rank list.
No compactness of all change-of-basis matrices is asserted.
This supplies the uniform step missing from a raywise account
of coercivity in HKKP Lemma~6.29.

\cite[Corollary~6 and Proposition~6(1)]{Poonen93MaximalAuthor}
supplies a complete maximally complete immediate extension.
Here the locators are checked in the nineteen-page author text.
Immediacy preserves the value group and residue field, and
hence the real-graded residue ring. Splitness after extension
is the standard maximally-complete-field norm fact, imported
in BE Lemma~1.12. Ordinary density before extension would not
give an exactly feasible split norm.

\subsection{What the reduction preserves}

For the graded maximal-slope block, the coboundary map has
no graded kernel. An orthogonal complement to its image
supplies a contractive left inverse. The strict residual-radius
bound then makes the nonlinear shear a contraction over complete
$K$. The remaining lower block lies in the kernel of this
left inverse. A nonzero such residual cannot be a coboundary,
so its reversed extension is genuinely nonsplit. A nonzero
lower block alone is insufficient: a shear can remove a
coboundary block. This is the additional condition needed
in the printed step of HKKP Proposition~7.4, supplied by
Ben-Bassat Lemma~4.9.

The reduction terminates by descent of the maximal slope and,
at a fixed slope, its maximal rank. Only finitely many rank
vectors occur. A later maximal rank can increase when the
slope has decreased, as in the two-radius example; lexicographic
descent still holds. The hinge construction in Lemma~5.3 and
the auxiliary quiver in Theorem~5.4 then transfer the minimum
back to the original ramp potential while preserving all
ordered arrow profiles.

The direct examples in
\eqref{eq:ch50-quiver-D4-bound}--\eqref{eq:ch50-quiver-two-radius-potentials}
retain their separate roles. The first gives a semistable
nonpolystable minimum and an unbounded set of minima modulo
common scaling. The second gives a nonsplit minimum alongside
a split one. The third gives a decreasing auxiliary potential
whose first step can increase the original potential. Zero
arrow profiles alone do not remove its determinant defect.
None is a claim that general semistability guarantees attainment,
that every minimum is split, or that the reduction is a monotone
algorithm for the original potential.

Source reading covers the complete Ben-Bassat v1, Poonen author file
and Moret-Bailly v3, plus selected BE, HKKP and Parreau pages.
External BGR, Bourbaki, EGA, flag-basis and CAT(0) completion
inputs remain attributed. This account claims no recursive
verification, original general theorem or independent-model receipt.
The forty-two atoms in twelve formal problems keep their statuses.
General non-Archimedean envelopes and BJ22's critical homogeneous
Siu division remain open; LPWZ density remains unverified.

\section{Transcendental approximation and base translations}
\label{sec:appL-transcendental-ytd-boundary}
\index{big test configuration!base translation}

Section~\ref{subsec:mpwn-special-kahler-passage} records a
conditional passage to the already normalized model inequality.
The controlling recent source is \cite{Trusiani26TranscendentalV1},
arXiv:\allowbreak 2609.31089v1, posted 25 September 2026 at
10:27:06 UTC. All sixteen actual PDF pages, including the
proofs and references, were read with eight individually displayed
matching images. This paper is distinct from the projective
August companion \cite{Trusiani26}. Its complete special-K\"ahler-Fujita
and YTD claims are not certified here.

The source requires convergence of the canonical trace in
addition to the top volume. The chapter keeps the fixed base
canonical trace, varying relative discrepancy, $J$ trace and
nonreduced-fiber correction separate. The limiting stability
inequality is an elementary conditional consequence of the
displayed hypotheses, not a new general theorem or an assertion
that the necessary approximants have been constructed.

The raw base-translation assertion on actual~13 fails outside
the normalized domain. Besides the existing Chapter~62 example,
take a smooth projective genus-two curve $C$ and blow up
$(x,0)\in C\times\mathbf P^1$. The standard base action fixes
$(x,0)$ and lifts to this blowup, which is unchanged off the
zero fiber. Let $H$ be the class of $\{x\}\times\mathbf P^1$,
$F$ the fiber class and $E$ exceptional.
Their intersection matrix is the one in
\Cref{ex:mpwn-big-translation-failure}.
Set $Q=F-E$, $T=H-E$ and $L=H+F-E$. The two classes have
Zariski decompositions
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 H+\tfrac32F-2E&=\tfrac12L+(Q+\tfrac12T),\\
 H+3F-2E&=(L+F)+Q.
 \end{aligned}
\]
The curves $Q$ and $T$ are disjoint with square $-1$.
For every other strict transform, its multiplicity at the blown-up
point is bounded by its intersection with each selected section
and fiber. Also $L\cdot E=1$. Hence $L$ is nef; $L^2=1$, and the negative parts
are orthogonal to their stated nef parts. The standard surface
Zariski identities of \cite{Bou04} give the moving volumes
$1/4$ and $3$. Here $V=1$, $K_{\cX/\mathbf P^1}=2H+E$,
and the zero fiber is reduced. Direct substitution gives
\begingroup
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.6}
\[
 \begin{array}{c|cc}
 &\DF&J^{\NA}\\ \hline
 H+\dfrac32F-2E&\dfrac54&\dfrac38\\
 H+3F-2E&2&\dfrac12
 \end{array}
\]
\endgroup
The shift is $\frac32F$ and its resulting vertical divisor
is $3Q+E\geq\cX_0$. The genus-two curve has trivial
automorphism identity component. Thus the normalization step
also needs correction within the source's automorphism domain;
these values do not refute its cscK conclusion.

The dependency reading includes twelve actual MPWN v1 pages:
1--2, 6, 16--20 and 31--34. Its mass and intersection
correspondences retain $f_D\geq1$; intrinsic NA functional
translation does not extend their raw correspondence to every
absolutely big class. Lemma~5.0.5 concerns a weakly convergent
NA sequence with bounded $J$ and a fixed finite-energy MA
measure; it does not alone construct the convergence needed
for arbitrary Fujita approximants. Six selected published
Boucksom pages, actual~1, 18 and~20--23, cover the surface
decomposition and volume statements, not the entire source.

All six published pages of \cite{Popovici04Regularization}
were also read. Lemma~1.2 is on printed~62, with proof on~63.
Its fixed-cover Bergman comparison gives the weight-independent
patching mechanism used in Trusiani Lemma~4.3. Operator and
weight conversion, finite-cover Jacobian bounds and holomorphic
transition multiplication on the overlap remain explicit; the
abstract's independence from the regularization index alone
would not give the required class uniformity. The standard
local multiplier-ideal and weighted $\bar\partial$ inputs are
attributed. The nonclosed $C^1$ variant is not used here.

The complete fourteen-page Tosatti v2 reading from the preceding
source audit is reused without fresh reading credit. Its
Corollary~1.3 supplies the general K\"ahler restricted-volume
trace and divisorial non-K\"ahler-locus criterion, with the earlier
external-proof qualifications retained. The August companion's
analytic discrepancy argument remains qualified; a normal
intermediate blowup need not admit ordinary canonical pullback.
A bounded-potential Weil-cycle projection is a proposed route,
not a certified repair. The exact DDL mixed-mass proof chain
was not freshly audited for this account.

The current source audit covers forty actual page pairs in twenty
individually displayed images: two complete files and two partial
selections. Prior Tosatti and projective-companion receipts are
not counted again. No new whole-book reading, original general
theorem, independent-model receipt or canonical status change
is claimed. The forty-two atoms in twelve formal problems keep
their statuses; general BJ22 critical homogeneous Siu division
and envelopes remain open, and LPWZ density remains unverified.

\section{Adiabatic projective bundles: coefficients and existence}
\label{sec:appL-adiabatic-bundles-boundary}
\index{projective bundle!adiabatic coefficient}

Section~\ref{subsec:mpwn-adiabatic-uniformity} separates eventual
stability in a family of classes from a uniform quantitative
margin. The controlling source is
\cite{OrtuSektnan24AdiabaticV2}, arXiv:2406.08284v2, its
accepted forty-five-page version posted on 3 October 2026 at
12:12:40 UTC. All forty-five actual pages were read in order,
with complete selected text, twenty-three individually displayed
matching images and sequential notes. This source reading does
not recursively verify the cited analytic inputs.

The hypotheses of its Theorem~1.1 include a simple bundle,
strict slope semistability, locally free stable equal-slope
Jordan--H\"older quotients, a smooth projective cscK base and
discrete polarized automorphisms. The Hermite--Einstein
connection is on the polystable graded bundle. The proof's
linear projection estimate in Lemma~3.5 and positivity of the
parameter-space form in Lemma~4.4 need further justification.
In particular, the written projection estimate does not by
itself provide its extra power of the adiabatic parameter, and
scalar line-bundle deformations can leave the projectivization
unchanged. The universal almost-complex moment-map and Mabuchi
transgression constructions supplied by the source address
different issues; they do not alone settle these two estimates.
The general equivalence is therefore retained as a qualified
source claim, not a theorem used elsewhere in the book.

Here is a reproducible diagnostic of its surface example. Let
$\dim B=2$, $\operatorname{rank}E=3$,
$\operatorname{rank}S=2$, and $Q=E/S$ a line bundle. Put
$s=c_1(S)$, $q=c_1(Q)$, $e=s+q$, $b=c_1(B)$,
$d=c_2(S)$, $z=c_2(E)=d+sq$, and $V=L^2$.
All products of base classes below mean their intersection
numbers. On $\mathbf P(S)$ the lines convention gives
\[
 \pi_*H=1,\qquad \pi_*H^2=-s,\qquad
 \pi_*H^3=s^2-d.
\]
The degree-three component of
$(1+3H)(1+H)^4/(1+H+q)$ is
\[
 10H^3-7H^2q+4Hq^2-q^3.
\]
The additional factor $b+e$ contributes
$(b+e)(3H^2-2Hq+q^2)$. Pushforward consequently gives
\[
 \delta_2=10(s^2-d)+7sq+4q^2-(3s+2q)(b+e).
\]
The specialized table on actual~38 instead prints $6sq+3q^2$.
Its preceding general finite sum gives the corrected coefficients
$7$ and $4$, so the difference is $qe$ and is not an overall
normalization choice.

The four corrected polynomials from its equation~(6.2) are
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \alpha&=6Vk^2-4(eL)k+e^2-z,\\
 \beta&=9Vk^2+3((b-2e)L)k-be+2e^2-3z,\\
 \gamma&=20Vk^2-5((3s+2q)L)k
                 +4(s^2-d)+3sq+2q^2,\\
 \delta&=24Vk^2+(8bL-20sL-8qL)k+\delta_2.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Set $P=(4\beta\gamma/5-\alpha\delta)/24$ and let
$a_2=[k^2]P$. If $sL/2=eL/3$, the product of the first-order
coefficients cancels, and direct expansion gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \frac{24a_2}{V}
 &=\frac25\bigl(-51s^2+78d+12se+26e^2-60z\bigr)\\
 &\quad+12\left(\frac{bs}{2}-\frac{be}{3}\right).
 \end{aligned}
\]
Compared with Lemma~6.5, the mixed and $e^2$ coefficients
are $12$ and $26$, rather than $-3$ and $41$. The difference
in $24a_2/V$ is $-6qe$.

A second check avoids the localization coefficient table.
On the central fiber $\mathbf P(S\oplus Q)$, use the lift
of weight $+1$ on $S$ and $0$ on $Q$; sections of
$\operatorname{Sym}^aS^*\otimes(Q^*)^{j-a}$ have weight $-a$.
The splitting principle gives, through base degree four,
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \operatorname{ch}(\operatorname{Sym}^aS^*)
 &=(a+1)-\frac{a(a+1)}2s\\
 &\quad+\frac{a(a+1)(2a+1)}{12}s^2
       -\frac{a(a+1)(a+2)}6d.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Multiply by $\exp(kjL-(j-a)q)\operatorname{Td}(B)$,
integrate, and sum over $0\leq a\leq j$ for the Hilbert
polynomial, or sum with weights $-a$ for the weight polynomial.
For a sufficiently large ample adiabatic polarization these
characteristics compute their leading Hilbert/weight coefficients.
Writing the coefficients as $A_0,A_1,B_0,B_1$ to distinguish
them from $a_2$, the result is
\[
 24A_0=\alpha,\quad 12A_1=\beta,\quad
 120B_0=-\gamma,\quad48B_1=-\delta.
\]
Thus
\[
 P=-48(A_1B_0-A_0B_1).
\]
In particular the explicitly chosen lift gives a positive DF
numerator when $P<0$. This dictionary resolves the apparent
sign conflict between a negative classical Futaki coefficient
and the source's positive-DF stability convention. Its literal
definition with denominator $A_0^2$ is invariant under tensoring
the polarization with generator fixed; reciprocal scaling stated
in Remark~6.10 needs a different specified normalization.

For the one-point blowup of $\mathbf P^2$, let $h$ denote
the pulled-back hyperplane class and $D$ the exceptional divisor.
Take $L=b=3h-D$, $s=0$, $q=h-3D$ and $z=d$.
Then $V=8$, $qL=bq=0$, $q^2=-8$, and
\[
 a_2=\frac{36d-416}{15}.
\]
The printed table gives $(36d-656)/15$. Both are negative
for $d=1$ or $2$; the correction does not alone reverse the
eventual sign. But a stable rank-two bundle on $\mathbf P^2$
with $s=0,d=1$ does not exist. Stability gives $h^0(S)=0$;
Serre duality and negative slope give
$h^2(S)=h^0(S^*(-3))=0$. Riemann--Roch gives
$\chi(S)=2-d=1$, contradicting $\chi(S)=-h^1(S)\leq0$.
The source's bundle-existence choice on actual~39 therefore
needs replacement independently of its coefficient correction.

For an actual nonzero extension of nonisomorphic stable
equal-slope $S$ and $Q$, simplicity does have a direct proof.
The two cross Hom spaces vanish. Every endomorphism of $E$
preserves $S$ and induces scalars $u,v$ on $S,Q$.
Compatibility with the nonzero extension class $\eta$ gives
$(u-v)\eta=0$, so $u=v$. Subtracting the scalar leaves a
map $Q\to S$, which vanishes. This conditional argument does
not construct a replacement bundle or cscK base. The primary
blowup, stability-flow, elliptic and moment-map dependencies
remain attributed and qualified. No general theorem is refuted
by these worked diagnostics.

The conditional ratio calculation in
\eqref{eq:mpwn-adiabatic-ratio-boundary} neither verifies the
source's minimum-norm coefficient nor establishes a uniform
stability theorem. This account supplies no independent-model
receipt or original general result. The forty-two atoms in
twelve formal problems keep their statuses: general BJ22
critical homogeneous Siu division and envelopes remain open,
and LPWZ density remains unverified.

\section{Adiabatic polarization scales and horizontal divisors}
\label{sec:appL-adiabatic-normalization-boundary}
\index{fibration!horizontal divisor}

The dictionary in \eqref{eq:mpwn-adiabatic-normalization} uses
\cite{Hattori23CalabiYauV2}, arXiv:2203.11460v2, updated on
9 August 2023. All forty-four pages were read in order with
complete selected text, twenty-two individually displayed
matching images and sequential notes. Metadata identifies the
2025 journal publication, DOI 10.1017/S1474748024000598, but
its proof text was not obtained; the observations below concern
the pinned arXiv version. Its general klt-trivial stability and
cscK equivalence remains a qualified source claim. This unit
uses its definitions and direct local calculations.

Write $d=\dim Y$, $V=P^d$, and let $\mathcal P$ be a model
polarization for $P$ on a normal semiample projective
configuration dominating the product, with a base twist
making the compactification nef. Hats in this section
denote the source's volume-normalized functionals; the model
intersection invariants in \eqref{eq:mpwn-df}--\eqref{eq:mpwn-model-j}
retain $V$. The normalized energy and $J$-functional are
\[
 \widehat E^{\NA}_P=\frac{\mathcal P^{d+1}}{(d+1)V},\qquad
 \widehat J^{\NA}_P=\frac{\mathcal P\cdot P_{\mathbf P^1}^{d}}{V}
             -\widehat E^{\NA}_P,
\]
with pullbacks on the common model understood. Rescaling both
$P$ and $\mathcal P$ by $\lambda>0$ multiplies $V$ by
$\lambda^d$ and both numerators by $\lambda^{d+1}$.
Thus $\widehat E^{\NA}$, $\widehat I^{\NA}$, $\widehat J^{\NA}$ and
$\widehat N^{\NA}:=\widehat I^{\NA}-\widehat J^{\NA}$ scale by $\lambda$.
For a fixed klt pair $(Y,\Delta)$, the normalized entropy is
\[
 \widehat H^{\NA}_\Delta
 =\frac{1}{V}\sum_T b_T A_{(Y,\Delta)}(v_T)
                      (T\cdot\mathcal P^d),
\]
where $b_T$ is the central-fiber multiplicity and $v_T$ the
induced valuation. It is invariant under this polarization
rescaling. The ordinary
relative-canonical term in $\widehat{\DF}$ is also invariant;
its canonical slope scales by $\lambda^{-1}$ and its energy
by $\lambda$. Hence $\widehat{\DF}_{\lambda P}=\widehat{\DF}_P$.
With $\Delta=0$, the unnormalized model DF is
$V\widehat{\DF}_P$ and scales by $\lambda^d$, whereas the
model $J^{\NA}=V\widehat J^{\NA}_P$ scales by $\lambda^{d+1}$.
Their ratio therefore agrees with the normalized ratio.
An auxiliary $\widehat J^D$ for fixed $D$ is invariant as well. These statements
concern polarization rescaling. Under normalized base change,
Mabuchi scales by the degree, while DF can decrease by the
nonreduced-central-fiber correction.

The printed proof of Theorem~A.6 on physical page~36 instead
uses the unnormalized degrees $\lambda^d$ for entropy and
$\lambda^{d+1}$ for the minimum norm. They do not agree with
its volume-normalized Definition~2.8. Here is the conditional
argument with the correct units. Assume the source's
Ding/threshold criterion gives $\widehat H^{\NA}_{rP}\geq\widehat N^{\NA}_{rP}$
whenever $\delta_{(Y,\Delta)}(rP)\geq1$.
For positive rational $r<\delta_{(Y,\Delta)}(P)$, homogeneity
of the threshold gives that premise and then
\[
 \widehat H^{\NA}_P=\widehat H^{\NA}_{rP}
 \geq\widehat N^{\NA}_{rP}=r\widehat N^{\NA}_P.
\]
Letting $r$ approach the threshold gives the intended coefficient.
This calculation leaves the Ding/threshold criterion assumed.

Another local arithmetic check concerns the curve-base volume
integral in Theorem~4.12, physical page~29. For a degree-one
fiber class $F$, put $a=A^{d-1}F$ and $b=A^d$. On $0<x<1$,
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \operatorname{vol}(\varepsilon A+(1-x)F)
   &=d(1-x)\varepsilon^{d-1}a+\varepsilon^d b,\\
 \int_0^1\operatorname{vol}(\varepsilon A+(1-x)F)\,dx
   &=\frac d2\varepsilon^{d-1}a+\varepsilon^d b.
 \end{aligned}
\]
The printed integral omits $d$. With the stated
$O(\varepsilon^{d+1})$ tail control, division by
$d\varepsilon^{d-1}a+\varepsilon^d b$ still gives the intended
limit $1/2$. The finite-basis comparison on the following page
also needs its reciprocal factor reversed before taking a limit.
These local repairs do not independently establish the general
$\alpha$- or $\delta$-convergence theorem.

There is a separate geometric issue in Lemma~4.8's proof on
physical pages~25--26: an incomplete vertical divisor can
intersect a fiber component negatively. Let $Z$ be the blowup
of $\mathbf P^1\times\mathbf P^1$ at one point, with ruling
classes $S,F$ and exceptional divisor $E_0$. Thus
\[
 S^2=F^2=0,\quad SF=1,\quad E_0^2=-1,
 \qquad F=E_0+\Gamma,\quad\Gamma=F-E_0.
\]
Here the last $F$ is the complete reducible fiber. The class
$A=2S+2F-E_0$ is ample: $A^2=7$, $AE_0=1$, and a different
irreducible strict transform of class $uS+vF$ and multiplicity
$m$ at the center has $m\leq u+v$ and intersection
$2(u+v)-m>0$. Apply the surface Nakai--Moishezon criterion.

Choose $P\in\Gamma\setminus E_0$ and smooth horizontal curves
$C_1\in|S+F|$, $C_2\in|S|$ meeting transversely at $P$ and
avoiding the blown-up point. For rational $\varepsilon>0$ put
\[
 D_{\rm hor}=\varepsilon(C_1+C_2),\qquad
 D_{\rm vert}=F+\varepsilon\Gamma.
\]
Both are effective, with the indicated horizontal and vertical
supports, and $D_{\rm hor}+D_{\rm vert}\sim_{\mathbf Q}
F+\varepsilon A$. Directly,
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 D_{\rm hor}\Gamma&=2\varepsilon,&
 \varepsilon A\Gamma&=\varepsilon,\\
 D_{\rm vert}\Gamma&=-\varepsilon,&
 \operatorname{mult}_P D_{\rm hor}&=2\varepsilon.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Thus the proof's equality dropping $D_{\rm vert}\Gamma$
is false. The example still satisfies a uniform horizontal
multiplicity bound with the complete-fiber budget
$\varepsilon AF=2\varepsilon$; it is not a counterexample to
the lemma or main equivalence.

The full source argument also invokes canonical-bundle and
moduli results, singularity bounds, equivariant minimal models,
filtered section algebras and analytic coercivity/existence.
Those external proofs were not recursively certified in this
unit. The projective-bundle scope boundary in
Section~\ref{subsec:mpwn-adiabatic-uniformity} remains explicit.
No new original general theorem or independent-model receipt
is introduced. The forty-two canonical atoms and twelve formal
problems retain their status: general BJ22 critical homogeneous
Siu division and envelopes remain open, and LPWZ density remains
unverified.

\section{Higher-rank integral bases and abeloid model interfaces}
\label{sec:appL-higher-rank-abeloid-boundary}
\index{abeloid variety!generically good reduction}
\index{Neron model@N\'eron model!marked formal model}

\Cref{sec:models-higher-rank-integral-data} uses
\cite{KaibWerner26HigherRankV1}, arXiv:2610.01354v1, posted on
1 October 2026. All twenty-four physical pages were read in order,
with complete selected text, twelve individually displayed matching
images and sequential notes. The source keeps $K$ complete and
nontrivially real-valued, with an additional valuation ring
$K^+\subset K^\circ$. Write $k=K^\circ/K^{\circ\circ}$ and
$V=K^+/K^{\circ\circ}$. The rank-one norm and the higher-rank
integral data play different roles. The present account is an attributed
interface and direct worked calculation; the external foundational
proofs are not recursively certified here.

\paragraph{Analytic recovery and integral gluing.}
Proposition~3.3 gives full faithfulness for partially proper adic spaces
under rank-one base change. Lemma~3.5 localizes the integral
subring of an affinoid pair at the elements of $K^+\cap(K^\circ)^\times$;
the topological ring and ordinary structure sheaf stay fixed, but the
integral structure sheaf need not. Lemma~3.8 reflects finite,
closed, proper and surjective maps with its partially proper hypotheses.
Lemma~3.9 retains the locally $+$weakly topologically finite-type assumptions
for its finite-type, flat, \'etale and smooth comparisons.
The single-localization condition in Remark~3.4 is an additional
hypothesis, not a universal description of every integral base.

Proposition~3.10 glues a formal $K^\circ$-model to a compatible
flat, locally finitely presented $V$-scheme along their $k$-reductions.
Its smooth/proper comparisons concern the entire $V$-reduction.
Proposition~3.12 then supplies the proper finite-type analytic equivalence;
its existence proof uses formal models and blowups, spreading over the
valuation spectrum, Nagata compactification and removal of valuation-ring
torsion. This is stronger than full faithfulness but does not remove the
additional choice of integral reduction for a prescribed formal model.
Huber's foundations, Fujiwara--Kato formal geometry and the compactification
inputs remain external to the elementary calculation in
\eqref{eq:models-composed-valuation}.

\paragraph{Uniformization and the good-reduction quotient.}
An abeloid is a smooth proper geometrically connected analytic group;
projectivity is an additional property. Theorem~4.7 passes to a finite
separable affinoid extension $(L,L^+)$, with $L^+\cap K=K^+$.
Its Raynaud cross is an exact sequence of analytic-site sheaves
\[
 0\longrightarrow T\longrightarrow E\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad A\simeq E/M,
\]
where $T$ is split, $M$ is a closed full lattice of its rank, and
$B$ is generically good. The universal-cover characterization uses
$H^1(E_{\rm an},\mathbf Z)=0$ and pointed connected test objects with
the same vanishing; it is not a statement of \'etale simple connectedness.
Classical rank-one uniformization and duality supply external inputs.

In Section~6, the marked formal model of the extension is built over
the open subgroup furnished by the formal model of $B$. Pushing out
its formal torus to $T$ first gives an extension $E'$ over that open
subgroup, and then the unique extension $E$ over the full proper $B$.
Corollary~6.9, after the prescribed choices, identifies good reduction of
$B$ with $E'=E$ and with constancy of the fiberwise torus rank in the
marked formal model. Generically good reduction alone does not supply
these conclusions. The elliptic equation in Chapter~3 exhibits the
corresponding integral obstruction through $j$ without invoking a
new general uniformization assertion.

\paragraph{Three mapping properties.}
A full algebraic N\'eron model in Section~5 is a smooth separated
model with the mapping property for all smooth algebraic test schemes;
it can be locally of finite type without being quasi-compact.
Proposition~5.3 instead gives a marked model for a semiabelian scheme
over a normal integral base, using pointed smooth quasi-separated
tests with connected fibers. The extension comparison of Lemma~5.5
requires a regular local base or a valuation ring with separably closed
fraction field, and a semiabelian model whose generic fiber is abelian.
It is not an unrestricted extension comparison for every torus quotient.

Theorem~5.7 assumes $V$ Henselian and every nongeneric residue field
algebraically closed. After its finite separable extension, gluing
translates of the semiabelian model supplies the full locally finite-type
N\'eron model. The algebraically closed fraction-field cases in
Corollary~5.10 and Lemma~5.11 have their own premises. These results do not assert
full-model existence over every valuation ring without qualifications.

Definition~6.3's marked \emph{formal} model models an open analytic
subgroup. Its formal fibers are connected, and its tests are pointed
smooth quasi-separated formal schemes with connected fibers. A strong
marked formal model is connected as a formal scheme, permits more than
one fiber component, and tests pointed smooth connected formal schemes.
Its maximal connected smooth formal open subgroup need not be the whole
proper abeloid. Theorem~6.7 gives a marked model after finite separable
affinoid extension; the strong conclusion assumes $k$ algebraically
closed or $V$ a discrete valuation ring. The finite-flat multiplication
statement of Proposition~6.12 keeps its distinct hypotheses: the formal
clause assumes $k$ algebraically closed and a semiabelian fiberwise identity
component; the algebraic clause assumes an algebraically closed fraction
field and a semiabelian generic fiber. It is not a statement for arbitrary
discrete-valuation N\'eron models.

\paragraph{Worked field and component groups.}
For $K=\mathbf Q_p((t))$ and $K^+=\mathbf Z_p+t\mathbf Q_p[[t]]$,
the residual valuation ring is the DVR $V=\mathbf Z_p$.
This fits the DVR alternative of Theorem~6.7 after its required
extension. The closed residue field $\mathbf F_p$ is not algebraically
closed, so the unmodified hypotheses of Theorem~5.7 do not hold.
For the curve in Chapter~3, $j=1/p$ belongs to $K^\circ\setminus K^+$.
Its discriminant is a $t$-adic unit but has $p$-order one. Modulo $p$
the equation becomes
\[
 y^2=(x+1)^2(x-2).
\]
At the node $x=-1$, the tangent cone is $y^2=-3(x+1)^2$;
it is split when $p=7$. The nodal calculation is a supplementary
check, not a converse to the necessary good-reduction criterion.

Example~6.14 of the source assumes $K$ algebraically closed for its
full elliptic classification and component formulas. Its algebraic
N\'eron model $N$ and strong marked formal model $\mathfrak N$ are
different objects; in general $\mathfrak N$ is not the completion of
$N$. For instance, over $K=\mathbf C_p$, with $K^+=K^\circ$ and a
Tate parameter $q$ of $p$-value $\ell>0$, the source's algebraic
component group is $\mathbf Q/\ell\mathbf Z$, whereas the strong
marked formal model has connected special fiber. The latter's component
group is zero, as the residual valuation ring is a field.
Neither formula identifies algebraic components with every real point
on the Berkovich skeleton circle. Confusing these mapping domains
would lose precisely the integral distinction the source studies.

The direct Laurent and elliptic calculations check the displayed
example, with the necessary $j$-integrality criterion attributed to
standard elliptic good reduction. They do not prove the source's
general N\'eron existence or uniformization results. No original
general theorem or independent-model receipt is introduced.
The forty-two canonical atoms and twelve formal problems retain their
status: general BJ22 critical homogeneous Siu division and envelopes
remain open, and LPWZ density remains unverified.

\section{Relative Hessian energies: boundary and total mass}
\label{sec:appL-relative-Hessian-boundary}
\index{Hessian equation!boundary normalization}
\index{Hessian equation!relative energy and total mass}

The normalization discussion in
\Cref{sec:ch51-KE-family-normalization} has a local counterpart:
bounded differences from a reference do not determine a Dirichlet trace,
and a weighted energy estimate need not control the unweighted total mass
near the boundary. These issues already arise for smooth references and
the one-factor Hessian operator.

The source under examination is
\cite{Truong26RelativeHessianV1}, arXiv:2609.12032v1, posted on
10 September 2026. All twenty-six physical pages, including the bibliography,
were read in order with complete selected text, thirteen individually
displayed matching images and sequential notes. The calculations below
test its literal definitions and selected statements; none of its general
relative Hessian theorems is imported. No later edition is substituted,
and the external bibliography proofs are not recursively certified here.

\paragraph{Operator and class conventions.}
In this section only, write
\[
 D_0=2\ddc=\frac{\rmi}{\pi}\partial\bar\partial,
 \qquad \beta_0=D_0|z|^2.
\]
Thus $D_0$ is the source's operator, twice the binding Chapter~51
operator in \eqref{eq:complex-ddc-normalization}.
On the unit disk $D\subset\mathbf C$, put $s=|z|^2$ and
$\psi=s-2$. For a radial function $f(s)$,
\[
 D_0f=(f'(s)+sf''(s))\beta_0,\qquad
 \int_Dg(s)\beta_0=2\int_0^1g(s)\,ds,\qquad
 \int_D\beta_0=2.
\]
All products used in the following tests are classical smooth products
on the open domain. The relative signed energy for one factor is
\[
 \mathcal E_\psi(f)=
 \frac12\int_D(f-\psi)(D_0f+D_0\psi).
\]
Definitions~2.4--2.5 on source physical pages~7--8 first require
$\psi-C\leq f\leq\psi$ for some $C>0$ and
$\int_DD_0f<\infty$, then take decreasing limits of such functions
with uniformly bounded absolute signed energy. No boundary trace is
specified. Smooth $\psi$ has analytic singularities with the trivial
holomorphic ideal, so this reference lies within the source's later
analytic-singularity assumption.

\paragraph{Comparison, injectivity and stability.}
Take
\[
 u=-3,\qquad v=s-3=\psi-1,\qquad w=s-4=\psi-2.
\]
Their differences from $\psi$ are $-1-s,-1,-2$, respectively.
All three are smooth negative members of the written basic class, and
\[
 D_0u=0,\qquad D_0v=D_0w=\beta_0,
\]
\[
 \mathcal E_\psi(u)=-\frac32,\quad
 \mathcal E_\psi(v)=-2,\quad
 \mathcal E_\psi(w)=-4.
\]
Membership in the decreasing-limit class can also be checked with
$u+1/j$, $v+1/j$ and $w+1/j$, rather than with stationary sequences.
These approximants stay below $\psi$, have finite mass and decrease
strictly to their respective limits. Their energies are, respectively,
$-3/2+1/j$, $-2+2/j$ and $-4+2/j$.

Now $\{u<v\}=D\setminus\{0\}$, so the comparison inequality in the
source's Theorems~1.3 and~5.1 would give
\[
 2=\int_{\{u<v\}}D_0v
 \ \leq\ \int_{\{u<v\}}D_0u=0.
\]
The boundary traces are $u=-3$, $v=-2$ and $\psi=-1$.
The usual boundary comparison premise has not been encoded by bounded
reference differences. Corollary~5.2's domination premise is vacuous
for $D_0u=0$, while its conclusion still fails on this example.
Corollary~5.3's injectivity is contradicted by $v\ne w$ with the same
nonzero measure. Equality of the two comparison integrals does not
force their common value to be zero.

The same pair tests the stated stability in Theorem~7.2:
let every $u_j=w$, let the target be $v$, and let
every $\mu_j=\mu=\beta_0$. The measures agree and the energies are
uniformly bounded, but $u_j-v=-1$ throughout $D$.
Also $\beta_0=(2/\pi)dV$ is an $L^\infty$ density, so the uniqueness
assertion of Theorem~7.3 fails under the written class definitions.
These are unnormalized local statements. They are not assertions
against a Dirichlet Hessian theory with its full boundary premises.

\paragraph{A nonzero Stokes boundary term.}
On the unit ball in $\mathbf C^2$, put $m=n=2$,
$\psi=|z|^2-2$, $u=\psi-1$, $v=\psi-2$ and $w_1=\psi$.
All functions belong to the written basic class, and
$\int\beta_0^2=4$. The source's global integration-by-parts
formula in Theorem~3.11 specializes to the two quantities
\[
 \int (u-v)D_0w_1\wedge D_0\psi=4,\qquad
 \int w_1D_0(u-v)\wedge D_0\psi=0.
\]
The difference is the boundary term: $u-v=1$ there as well as inside.
Physical page~13 discards this term using relative boundary vanishing
that was never imposed. Compactly supported local integration by parts
has a different domain and is not disputed by this calculation.

\paragraph{Common trace, finite energy and infinite mass.}
A second test retains the boundary trace. On the disk with the same
strictly subharmonic $\psi$, set $\alpha=3/4$ and
\[
 h_\epsilon(s)=-(1-s+\epsilon)^\alpha+\epsilon^\alpha,\qquad
 f_\epsilon=\psi+h_\epsilon,\qquad \epsilon>0.
\]
Concavity of $x^\alpha$ gives $-1\leq h_\epsilon\leq0$,
and $h_\epsilon(1)=0$. Moreover,
\[
 h_\epsilon'=\alpha(1-s+\epsilon)^{\alpha-1}>0,\qquad
 h_\epsilon''=\alpha(1-\alpha)(1-s+\epsilon)^{\alpha-2}>0.
\]
Thus $f_\epsilon$ is subharmonic and smooth up to the boundary for
each positive $\epsilon$. Since
$\partial_\epsilon h_\epsilon
=\alpha(\epsilon^{\alpha-1}-(1-s+\epsilon)^{\alpha-1})>0$
for $s<1$, the sequence $f_{1/j}$ decreases to
$f=\psi-(1-s)^\alpha$.
Radial integration gives
\[
 \int_DD_0f_\epsilon
 =2+2\alpha\epsilon^{\alpha-1}
 =2+\frac32\epsilon^{-1/4}.
\]
For the signed energy, integration by parts in the radial coordinate
is legitimate at each positive $\epsilon$: the boundary product
$s h_\epsilon h_\epsilon'$ is zero at both endpoints. Hence
\[
 \mathcal E_\psi(f_\epsilon)
 =2\int_0^1h_\epsilon(s)\,ds
  -\alpha^2\int_0^1s(1-s+\epsilon)^{2\alpha-2}\,ds.
\]
The first term has absolute value at most $2$. The second has absolute
value at most
\[
 \alpha^2\int_0^1s(1-s)^{2\alpha-2}\,ds
 =\frac{\alpha}{2(2\alpha-1)}=\frac34.
\]
Consequently $|\mathcal E_\psi(f_{1/j})|\leq11/4$, and direct
integration yields
\[
 \lim_{\epsilon\downarrow0}\mathcal E_\psi(f_\epsilon)
 =-\frac{2}{\alpha+1}-\frac{\alpha}{2(2\alpha-1)}
 =-\frac{53}{28}.
\]
Every approximant has finite global mass, while these masses tend to
infinity. The limit is smooth in the open disk and has a locally finite
Hessian measure, but its total mass is infinite. It nevertheless
belongs to the source's decreasing finite-energy class by the explicit
approximants just given. Since $f\geq\psi-1$,
\[
 \max(f,\psi-k)=f\qquad(k\in\mathbf Z,\ k\geq1).
\]
None of these canonical truncations belongs to the basic
finite-global-mass class. Lemma~3.6's membership assertion therefore
fails as printed, even after imposing this common boundary trace.
A weighted energy bound cannot supply the unweighted global mass bound
used in the later product argument. This does not refute local
one-factor continuity: $L^1_{\rm loc}$ convergence still gives
distributional convergence of $D_0f_j$ against smooth compactly
supported tests.

\paragraph{Constant shifts and admissible data.}
Section~6 on physical page~20 changes the class description to an upper
bound by $\psi+C$ and finite signed energy. On the disk, every
$\phi=\psi-C$, $C>0$, has $\mathcal E_\psi(\phi)=-2C$.
Theorem~6.1(ii), applied to every such shift, requires
\[
 C\mu(D)\leq A(2C)^{1/2}\qquad(C>0),
\]
so division by $C$ and passage to infinity force $\mu(D)=0$.
Thus this unnormalized condition cannot admit nonzero finite data in
the smooth example. The positive-energy function $\psi+C$ is also
permitted by that section's upper-bound definition; for $0<C<1$ it is
still negative, whereas $(-\mathcal E_\psi(\psi+C))^{1/2}$
is not real. No claim is made that the $L^\infty$ density used above
satisfies this separate hypothesis.

With a singular reference, relative boundedness need not imply ordinary
local boundedness. Defining mixed products, controlling weighted tails
and changing test coefficients, preserving measure hypotheses under
smoothing, and passing beyond decreasing sequences require separate
arguments. These tests establish no general existence theorem.

\section{Uniform bounds and Calabi--Yau finiteness}
\label{sec:appL-polarized-CY-finiteness-boundary}
\index{Calabi--Yau manifold!uniformity and finiteness}
\index{Bergman kernel!sum of section suprema}

The finiteness programme in \cite{Truong26PolarizedCYFinitenessV1}
links complex Hessian capacity, noncollapse, regularity, metric
compactness and algebraic boundedness. Its final discussion explicitly
leaves the capacity--perimeter and metric-to-projective-embedding
steps to further work. The following tests separate those inputs from
the elementary and classical conditional implications between them.
All observations concern the pinned thirty-four-page version~1,
posted 30 September 2026.

For this source, \(dd^c_{\rm src}=i\partial\bar\partial
=2\pi dd^c\), where the last operator is the book convention.
Use Riemannian volume for metric ball estimates. For a polarized
K\"ahler form \(\eta\) with \([\eta]=2\pi c_1(L)\),
\[
 \operatorname{Vol}_{g_\eta}(M)=\frac1{n!}\int_M\eta^n,\qquad
 \int_Mc_1(L)^n=\frac1{(2\pi)^n}\int_M\eta^n.
\]
These factors must be retained when a geometric bound is converted
to a projective degree.

\paragraph{Capacity estimates and compatible exponents.}
Theorem~3.1 on physical pages~6--8 assumes a uniform inequality
\(v(E)\leq C_0\operatorname{Cap}(E)^\tau\) for all Borel sets.
Its conclusion follows with \(C=C_0\) and exponent \(\tau\).
It supplies no construction of that assumed family-uniform estimate.
Theorem~4.1's H\"older step gives the sublevel-measure exponent
\[
 q=\frac p{p-1},\qquad \delta=\frac{\tau}{q}.
\]
The displayed iteration needs \(\delta>1\).
An estimate for one specified \(\tau\) does not allow that exponent
to be increased at will. For \(n=3,m=1,p=4,\tau=6/5\), all printed
strict bounds hold, but \(\delta=9/10\).
The page~11 truncation also fails its claimed value on the deeper
sublevel: at \(s=1,t=2,u=-5/2\),
\[
 \max\{(u+t)/(t-s),-1\}=-1/2\ne-1,\qquad u<-t.
\]
On \(u\geq-s\) it is at least \(1\), so it is not a capacity test
in \([-1,0]\). A corrected comparison must check every admissible
test and the background subharmonicity constraint.
These are defects in the printed proof, rather than a counterexample
to every possible repaired potential estimate.

There is also a purely algebraic exponent test for the proposed
capacity--perimeter bridge. On the same class of sufficiently regular
domains of at most half the total volume, suppose separately that
\[
 v\leq A c^\tau,\qquad c\leq B b^\sigma,\qquad v\leq V_0,
\]
with \(A,B,V_0,\sigma,\tau>0\), where \(v,c,b\) denote volume,
capacity and boundary area. Zero-volume domains give the trivial
inequality. For \(v>0\), put \(\rho=\sigma\tau\). Substitution gives
\[
 v^{1/\rho}\leq A^{1/\rho}B^{1/\sigma}b.
\]
In real dimension \(d>1\), if \(\rho\geq d/(d-1)\), multiplication
by \(v^{(d-1)/d-1/\rho}\) gives
\[
 v^{(d-1)/d}
 \leq V_0^{(d-1)/d-1/\rho}A^{1/\rho}B^{1/\sigma}b.
\]
No capacity--perimeter estimate has been proved by this substitution.
The half-volume restriction is essential on a compact manifold:
the formula with all domains would fail for \(E=M\), whose boundary
is empty and volume positive.

\paragraph{Noncollapse and the scale in a diameter bound.}
Theorem~4.6 on physical pages~13--15 assumes a uniform critical
Sobolev inequality. In real dimension \(d=2n\), \(n\geq2\), let its
constant be \(S\), let a ball cutoff have gradient at most \(G/r\),
and assume the classical Ricci-nonnegative volume doubling.
Writing \(V(r)=\operatorname{Vol}_g B_g(x,r)\), its argument gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 V(r)^{(d-2)/d}
 &\leq S(G^2r^{-2}+1)V(2r)\\
 &\leq S2^d(G^2+1)r^{-2}V(r)\qquad(0<r\leq1).
 \end{aligned}
\]
Division therefore yields
\[
 V(r)\geq [S2^d(G^2+1)]^{-d/2}r^d.
\]
The extra potential bounds are unused in this calculation.
Classical cutoff and Bishop--Gromov inputs are being used in
their stated scopes; a uniform Sobolev bound has not been obtained
from polarization data alone.

Theorem~5.1's proof reverses Bishop--Gromov for a radius \(R>r_0\).
The correct direction is
\[
 \frac{V(R)}{R^d}\leq\frac{V(r_0)}{r_0^d}.
\]
The printed numerical diameter bound consequently loses essential
scale information. A flat rectangular elliptic torus with sides
\(1/10\) and \(10\) has area \(1\), zero Ricci curvature, and
\[
 \operatorname{diam}M=\frac12\sqrt{100+1/100}>5.
\]
Every ball of radius at most \(r_0=1/40\) has area \(\pi r^2\).
The printed bound is \(2/\sqrt\pi<1.13\), so it fails even on
this smooth example. This does not refute a diameter bound depending
on the noncollapse scale.

Here is a direct argument retaining that scale. On a connected
compact Riemannian manifold, assume
\(\operatorname{Vol}_g M\leq V_0\) and
\(\operatorname{Vol}_g B_g(x,r_0)\geq\kappa r_0^d\) for every \(x\).
Along a minimizing geodesic of length \(\ell\), the
\(\lfloor\ell/(2r_0)\rfloor+1\) open radius-\(r_0\) balls with centres
spaced \(2r_0\) apart are disjoint. Their volumes add to at most \(V_0\).
Since their number is greater than \(\ell/(2r_0)\),
\[
 \ell<\frac{2V_0}{\kappa r_0^{d-1}},\qquad
 \operatorname{diam}M\leq\frac{2V_0}{\kappa r_0^{d-1}}.
\]
This packing implication does not require the reversed comparison.
Curvature and its derivatives remain separate inputs for the source's
smooth compactness statement, Theorem~6.1. Its generic regularity
argument also needs the actual derivative and H\"older bounds used
by the PDE estimates, rather than an unspecified low-order density
norm. Under smooth compactness with eventual diffeomorphisms, infinitely
many distinct diffeomorphism types would contradict a convergent
subsequence. That topological consequence already precedes projective
embedding.

\paragraph{A Bergman upper bound and individual suprema.}
Lemma~7.1 is stated for general compact K\"ahler manifolds.
On \(\mathbf P^1\) with \(\mathcal O(1)\), use the Fubini--Study
metric and its volume measure rescaled to total mass \(V\) for
the \(L^2\) inner product. Put
\[
 a([z_0:z_1])=\frac{|z_0|^2}{|z_0|^2+|z_1|^2}.
\]
The standard orthonormal basis has squared pointwise norms
\((2/V)a\) and \((2/V)(1-a)\): the two unnormalized diagonal
integrals are \(V/2\), and the angular cross integral is zero.
Thus
\[
 \sup_x\sum_{\alpha=0}^1|s_\alpha(x)|^2=\frac2V,\qquad
 \sum_{\alpha=0}^1\sup_x|s_\alpha(x)|^2=\frac4V.
\]
This contradicts the lemma's stated constant; its proof admits the
missing implication and adds a stronger section hypothesis.
It is not a Calabi--Yau counterexample.

If a pointwise Bergman sum is bounded by \(C_B\), the basis is
\(L^2\)-orthonormal, and the same measure has total mass at most \(V_0\),
integration does give \(h^0\leq C_BV_0\). Each section has squared
supremum at most \(C_B\), so the resulting sum bound is
\(C_B^2V_0\), rather than \(C_B\). This gives no common very ample
power, Bergman lower bound or tangent separation.
For a basis without orthonormality, a scalar rescaling can make
its supremum sum arbitrarily small while leaving the projective
map unchanged.

\paragraph{The scheme scope and the final topological step.}
Lemma~7.2 correctly gives
\(\deg\Phi_{L^q}(M)=q^n\int_Mc_1(L)^n\), assuming \(L^q\) very ample.
Theorem~7.3 assumes a common \(q\), fixed section dimension and bounded
degree; it does not derive these from metric estimates.
Its powered polarizations can use an appropriate classical boundedness
input for smooth pure-dimensional projective varieties.

Lemma~8.1, however, asserts boundedness for arbitrary projective schemes
from dimension, ambient space and top degree alone.
Take a linear \(\mathbf P^3\subset\mathbf P^4\), and add \(k\) disjoint
reduced points outside it. Their disjoint union \(X_k\) has dimension
\(3\) and top degree \(1\), but
\[
 P_{X_k}(t)=\binom{t+3}{3}+k,\qquad
 h^0(X_k,\mathcal O_{X_k})=1+k.
\]
The Hilbert polynomials are not finite in number.
The degree-zero homogeneous coordinate ring still has dimension \(1\),
so it cannot be identified with this \(h^0\) in every degree.
Gotzmann regularity does not bound arbitrary lower-dimensional or
embedded contributions from the leading coefficient alone.
This example does not refute a correctly restricted boundedness
statement for smooth connected pure-dimensional varieties.

Once finitely many Hilbert polynomials are actually available,
Theorem~8.3's topological conclusion can use the universal projective
flat families and a finite smooth stratification of the reduced bases.
Restricting to connected smooth strata in the locus of smooth fibres
gives proper smooth submersions, to which classical Ehresmann applies.
The open locus of smooth fibres need not itself be a complex manifold.
Hilbert-scheme, stratification and Ehresmann foundations remain
attributed inputs. The resulting conditional chain establishes no
unconditional finiteness theorem from the Calabi--Yau and polarization
conditions alone.

\section{Local psh growth and the scope of envelopes}
\label{sec:appL-local-psh-growth-boundary}
\index{psh functions!local growth}
\index{envelopes!weak psh descent}

Wu's \emph{On the local theory of non-archimedean psh functions}
\cite{Wu26LocalNApshV1} proposes a local psh class given by decreasing
tropical convex PL presentations. The controlling version was posted
on 23 September 2026. Theorem~4.17 compares it with global model-theoretic psh
functions on a projective variety under an envelope-property hypothesis.
Theorems~6.3 and~6.5 propose weak-psh comparison over complete discretely
or trivially valued fields of equicharacteristic zero with a countable
dense subfield. The latter assumes an ample rational polarization.
Theorem~7.1 then claims the envelope property for geometrically unibranch
projective varieties, including normal varieties, without the countability
restriction. Appendix~A compares the local class with a specifically
defined smooth-approximable CLD class.

The complete fifty-five-page version was reviewed with matching text
and rendered pages. The preceding claims are recorded with their
dependency boundaries: this book does not use the general weak/global
comparison or envelope theorem from that version. The following
one-variable calculations explain some of the necessary distinctions.
They are elementary diagnostics of the printed definitions and proof
interfaces; they do not establish that an intended corrected main
theorem is false.

\paragraph{Upper growth and coercive lower growth.}
On an affine variety \(Y\), the source calls a function Fubini--Study
when it has the form
\[
 h=\max\{c_i\log|f_i|+C_i:1\leq i\leq N;\ \lambda\},
 \qquad c_i\in\mathbf Q_{>0},\quad C_i,\lambda\in\mathbf Q,
\]
where the \(f_i\) generate \(K[Y]\). The finite floor handles common
zeros. On \(\mathbf A^1\), at least one generator has positive degree,
so every such \(h\) tends to infinity along Gauss points of radii
tending to infinity. It is also eventually bounded above by
\(A\log|T|+B\), because only finitely many polynomial terms occur.

Definition~3.15(2) prints a lower-growth condition, whereas
Definition~6.1(2) prints an upper-growth condition:
\[
 h\leq u+O(1)
 \qquad\hbox{and}\qquad
 u\leq h+O(1),
 \quad\hbox{respectively}.
\]
The first is a coercive lower bound; the second is the usual type of
upper logarithmic control. They are different. The later proofs use
upper majorants, so replacing one inequality with the other requires a
consistent revision of the definitions and all dependent statements.

For example,
\[
 v_j(T)=\max\{\log|T|-j,0\}\downarrow0.
\]
Each \(v_j\) is Fubini--Study. The limit is finite and locally psh,
but cannot dominate an affine Fubini--Study function up to a constant.
Thus the lower-growth class is not closed under these decreasing
limits. This contradicts the literal lower-growth conclusions of
Corollary~3.18 and Lemmas~3.24(3), 3.25(3), while leaving a consistently
formulated upper-growth theory possible. A decreasing global
Fubini--Study presentation always gives an upper majorant, namely its
first member. The source's Definition~3.15(3) additionally retains
its printed lower-growth premise.

\paragraph{A local psh function with no linear upper majorant.}
Write \(t=\log|T|\) for \(T\ne0\), and set
\[
 w(T)=\max_{k\in\mathbf Z_{\geq0}}\{kt-k^2\},\qquad w(0)=0.
\]
On a region \(t\leq R\), every term with \(k>\max(R,0)\) is negative
and is dominated by the \(k=0\) term. Consequently the maximum is
locally finite, continuous and convex in the radial coordinate. Near
\(T=0\) it is identically zero. It therefore supplies a local convex
PL psh function under the genuine affine-support convention.
Its first two terms give
\[
 \max(t,0)\leq w+1.
\]
For each integer \(m\geq0\), at the Gauss point \(\eta_{e^{2m}}\),
\[
 w(\eta_{e^{2m}})
 =\max_{k\geq0}\{2mk-k^2\}
 =\max_{k\geq0}\{m^2-(k-m)^2\}
 =m^2.
\]
No fixed \(At+B\) dominates this sequence. In particular \(w\)
has the printed Fubini--Study lower bound but no Fubini--Study upper
majorant, and hence no decreasing global Fubini--Study presentation.
The first majorant step in Proposition~4.19 does not follow from its
printed lower-growth hypothesis. That proof also applies a
regularizable-class extension lemma before obtaining membership in
that class.

\paragraph{Local curvature does not imply finite total mass.}
Choose a nontrivially discretely valued field normalized by
\(-\log|\pi|=1\). Along the radial Gauss ray, the active slope of \(w\)
changes from \(k\) to \(k+1\) at \(t=2k+1\).
With the unit calibration and sign convention of
\Cref{sec:ch19-graph-laplacian}, this gives on the affine line
\[
 \ddc w=\sum_{k\geq0}\delta_{\eta_{e^{2k+1}}}.
\]
Every compact subset is contained in a bounded-radius set and meets
only finitely many of these atoms. The curvature is therefore a
positive Radon measure, but its total mass is infinite. The constant
function \(1\), although locally bounded and psh, is not globally
\(L^1\) for this measure.

This is the distinction between an \(L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}\) conclusion
and an \(L^1(U,\mu)\) conclusion on a noncompact open set. Theorem~4.2(3)
prints the latter, while its local argument provides the former.
The calculation does not reject local Bedford--Taylor products,
compact-carrier estimates or convergence tested against compactly
supported functions. Global integrability and global mass control
require additional hypotheses.

Generic finiteness is another separate condition. Constants \(-j\)
are psh and decrease to the excluded identically minus-infinity
function. A decreasing-limit statement must either require a
generically finite limit or expressly allow that exceptional function.
Corollary~4.18(1) does include this premise; its clause~(4) and
Proposition~2.21 omit it. Similarly the whole product
\(D=Y^{\An}\times\mathbf A^{1,\An}\) is Runge and contains the zero
section, but its vertical radius is infinity everywhere.
Proposition~5.7 therefore needs a nondegeneracy condition before the
negative logarithm of that radius belongs to a psh class excluding
the identically minus-infinity function.

\paragraph{Strict norm bounds and actual pole orders.}
For a compact polynomial hull, each defining polynomial keeps its
supremum norm bound. Strict finite polynomial sublevels can therefore
be handled by uniform strict bounds on their compact carriers.
The interior of an arbitrarily presented polytope, however, need not
equal the set obtained by making every displayed inequality strict.
Present \(P=\{|T|\leq1\}\) also with the redundant constraint
\(|1|\leq1\). Its interior contains \(\{|T|<1\}\), but tightening
all constraints by any positive amount makes the constant constraint
impossible. This diagnoses Lemma~5.3's simultaneous shrinking
identity, rather than polynomial convexity or a corrected Runge
neighbourhood theorem.

The localization argument in Lemma~5.11 uses a more substantial
algebraic implication: \(g\notin(f)\) is said to provide a point of
\(V(f)\) where \(g\ne0\). This is not true for a nonradical ideal.
On \(\mathbf A^1\), take
\[
 f=T^2,\quad g=T,\quad r=1,\quad c_1=1,\quad c=\tfrac12,\quad
 u=\max(\log|T|,0).
\]
On \(Y_f=\mathbf G_m\), the Fubini--Study function
\[
 u_c=\max\{u,-c\log|f|\}
     =\max\{\log|T|,-\log|T|,0\}
\]
contains the term \(\log|g/f|=-\log|T|\). It satisfies the required
upper bound exactly, although \(c_1r=1>c\), contrary to the claimed
formal denominator-power estimate. Here \(g\notin(T^2)\) but vanishes
at every point of \(V(T^2)\). A corrected proof must control actual
vanishing and pole orders, or impose hypotheses that justify that
control. This example does not itself refute an independently proved
localization inequality.

\paragraph{The remaining envelope dependency.}
Theorem~7.1 pulls envelopes to a smooth resolution, uses connected
fibres and fibre constancy to descend an upper semicontinuous
function, and then invokes Theorem~6.5 to identify the descendant as
globally psh. The identification is essential. Besides the growth
and localization issues above, the finite-surjective norm proof
in Proposition~6.12 applies Proposition~4.19 over a completed residue
field \(H(s)\). The latter proposition assumes a discretely or
trivially valued field, whereas \(H(s)\) can be nondiscrete:
the Gauss valuation with \(v(\pi)=1\) and \(v(T)=\sqrt2\) has value
group containing \(\mathbf Z+\sqrt2\mathbf Z\).
The arbitrary-field curve limsup lemma used later supplies a
uniqueness step, not that missing regularization input.
The local branch-multiplicity argument also needs a proved common
rank on the chosen neighbourhood; continuity and integer values
alone give local constancy. Its estimate must separately handle
the possible multiplicity \(\gamma=1\).

Appendix~A's smooth-approximable comparison uses two essential
Amini--Piquerez--Wu tropical preprints listed without versions or
identifiers, and requires decreasing approximation on a fixed
neighbourhood. Those cited proofs are not recursively certified by
reading this paper. Corrected signs and conventional maximum
identities do not replace these inputs. The general envelope claim
is consequently not used to change the book's open-problem ledger,
to repair BJ22 Theorem~6.21, or to certify LPWZ density.
No original general theorem or new independent-model receipt is
asserted by these elementary calculations.

\section{Model Green currents and finite flat norm metrics}
\label{sec:appL-model-green-norm-scope}
\index{Green currents!regular model scope}
\index{norm metrics!finite flat morphisms}

Two constructions clarify different interfaces in
\Cref{chap:cld-metrized-bundles,sec:ch19-common-core}.
Dubocs \cite{Dubocs26GreenV1} works with algebraic currents defined
through model Chow groups. Boucksom--Eriksson
\cite[Proposition~8.22]{BE21} work with norms of continuous global
psh metrics. Their scopes must be retained before either construction
is used in a local analytic comparison. The complete twenty-seven-page
Dubocs version and twenty-nine selected pages of the published
Boucksom--Eriksson article were read with matching text and rendered
images. The earlier classical inputs cited below have not thereby
been recursively verified.

\paragraph{The model current category.}
Let \(R\) be an excellent discrete valuation ring with fraction field
\(K\), and let \(X\) be proper smooth over \(K\), of pure dimension
\(n\). The models in \cite[\S1]{Dubocs26GreenV1} are regular proper
flat \(R\)-schemes with generic fibre \(X\); existence and common
domination of the required regular models are standing hypotheses.
For a model \(\mathcal X\), write \(i:\mathcal X_0\hookrightarrow
\mathcal X\) and
\[
 E_{\mathcal X}:\mathrm{CH}^\bullet(\mathcal X)
 \longrightarrow \mathrm{CH}_\bullet(\mathcal X),
 \qquad c\longmapsto c\cap[\mathcal X].
\]
Here \(\mathrm{CH}^\bullet\) is operational Chow cohomology.
Poincar\'e duality is used on the regular total model, not on an
arbitrary singular special fibre. The forms and currents include
\[
 A_c^{k,k}(X)=\varinjlim_{\pi_0^*}\mathrm{CH}^k(\mathcal X_0),
 \qquad
 \widetilde D^{k,k}(X)
   =\varprojlim_{\pi_{0*}}\mathrm{CH}_{n-k}(\mathcal X_0).
\]
The operational current group \(D_c^{k,k}\) instead uses
operational Chow groups and their appropriate proper pushforwards.
In this category the differentiation map on a model is
\[
 dd^c_{\mathrm{alg}}
 =i^*\circ E_{\mathcal X}^{-1}\circ i_*,
 \qquad
 (\delta_Z)_{\mathcal X}
 =i^*E_{\mathcal X}^{-1}[\overline Z].
\]
The second formula takes the scheme cycle with its multiplicities.
A form can act on a current through operational intersection.
The source supplies no product of two arbitrary currents and no
natural restriction to every open subset of the generic variety.
Its \(dd^c_{\mathrm{alg}}\) is not identified by notation alone
with this book's CLD differential.

\paragraph{Rank one and section-ideal coefficients.}
A metric in this model category is a vector-bundle model modulo
common-model pullback. Induced and quotient metrics are formed on a
dominating model where the quotient is locally free; their Chern
forms satisfy the model Whitney identity. For a line bundle with
such a metric and a nonzero Cartier section, the source proves
\cite[Proposition~2.6]{Dubocs26GreenV1}
\[
 dd^c_{\mathrm{alg}}\operatorname{div}_v(s)
 +\delta_{\operatorname{div}(s)}
 =[c_1(\mathcal L)].
\]
The vertical cycle is the vertical part of the meromorphic extension's
divisor. The proof cancels the horizontal closure and uses
\(c_1(\mathcal L)\cap[\mathcal X]
=[\operatorname{div}(s_{\mathcal X})]\).
Its sign is opposite to the \(\operatorname{div}_\nu\) convention
of the earlier model-current source, as the preprint explicitly notes.
An analogy with \(-\log\|s\|^2\) does not fix an analytic comparison:
the normalization of both differentials and a comparison map are
still needed. The minus-log conventions of
\Cref{app:normalizations} are retained.

Because \(\dim\mathcal X_0=n\), top-dimensional cycles have
\(\mathrm{CH}_n(\mathcal X_0)=\mathrm Z_n(\mathcal X_0)\).
For a proper birational map between regular models, each target
divisor has a unique degree-one strict transform and contracted
divisors push forward to zero. It follows that coefficientwise
minimum commutes with these top-cycle transition maps. This defines
a minimum on \(\widetilde D^{0,0}\), not on all current degrees.
For two distinct rational points above the same closed point of an
exceptional projective line, the minimum is zero before pushforward,
whereas both pushforwards are the same point cycle.

If a vector section has components \(f_1,\ldots,f_r\) in a model
frame, its coefficient at a vertical divisor \(V\) is
\[
 \min_j\operatorname{ord}_V(f_j),
 \qquad \operatorname{ord}_V(0)=+\infty.
\]
Integral frame transitions and their integral inverses give the two
opposite inequalities that prove independence of the frame.
Overlap-compatible cycles on model opens, compatible with proper
pushforward on every dominating model, then give the section-ideal
current on \(X\). This is a lattice of vertical divisorial
coefficients, not a psh-envelope construction.

\paragraph{A regular section can have a singular generic blowup.}
The higher-rank construction forms
\(\pi:\widetilde X=\operatorname{Bl}_Z X\to X\), uses
\(\mathbf P(F)=\operatorname{Proj}\operatorname{Sym}(F^\vee)\),
and pulls the tautological line back to \(\mathcal O_{\widetilde X}(E)\).
The canonical section \(s\) maps to \(\pi^*\sigma\) under this line's
inclusion into \(\pi^*F\). The announced Green current is
\[
 \Lambda=\pi_*\bigl(\operatorname{div}_v(s)
          \wedge f^*c_{r-1}(Q_F)\bigr).
\]
The proof in \cite[\S4]{Dubocs26GreenV1} applies the smooth-generic
current operations and the inverse Poincar\'e duality of a raw model
blowup. Neither regularity requirement follows from a regular section.

Take \(K=\mathbf Q((t))\), \(X=\mathbf P^2_K\) and
\(F=\mathcal O(2)\oplus\mathcal O(2)\), with section \((X^2,Y^2)\).
Near \([0:0:1]\), the ideal is the regular sequence \((x^2,y^2)\).
Its quotient has basis \(1,x,y,xy\) and length four.
One blowup chart is
\[
 K[x,y,u]/(y^2-ux^2).
\]
At \((x,y,u)=(0,0,1)\), put \(w=u-1\). The local equation
\(y^2-(1+w)x^2\) has no linear term. The local ring has dimension
two and embedding dimension three, hence is singular.
A regular \(R\)-scheme cannot have this exact generic fibre,
since regularity localizes. Regular-model cofinality for proper
smooth generic schemes therefore does not cover this blowup.

There is also a separate total-model issue. On
\(\mathbf P^2_{\mathbf Q[[t]]}\), the section \((tX,Y)\) of
\(\mathcal O(1)\oplus\mathcal O(1)\) has a smooth generic zero point.
A chart of its raw total blowup is
\[
 \mathbf Q[[t]][x,y,v]/(tx-vy).
\]
At \(t=x=y=v=0\), the local ring has dimension three and embedding
dimension four. After inverting \(t\), however, \(x=vy/t\) gives
the smooth generic chart \(K[y,v]\). Thus a smooth generic centre
does not make the particular raw total model regular.

These examples identify an unprovided premise or construction in the
printed proof. They do not refute an intended Green identity after
a suitable principalization and compatible regular-model construction.
Such a repair must also prove the induced-metric and exceptional-cycle
pushforward identities on the new system. The general higher-rank
formula is not used here as a theorem.

\paragraph{Split Chern coefficients and the local/global boundary.}
For a genuinely split model metric on \(F=L^{\oplus r}\), the
formal coefficient identity used in the source is
\[
 \sum_{j=k}^{r-1}
 \binom r{r-1-j}\binom jk(-1)^{j-k}=1,
 \qquad 0\leq k\leq r-1.
\]
Indeed,
\[
 \sum_{j\geq k}\binom jk z^j(-1)^{j-k}
 =\frac{z^k}{k!}(-1)^k
   \frac{d^k}{dz^k}(1+z)^{-1}
 =\frac{z^k}{(1+z)^{k+1}},
\]
so multiplication by \((1+z)^r\) leaves
\(z^k(1+z)^{r-1-k}\), whose \(z^{r-1}\) coefficient is one.
The factor \((-1)^k\), omitted in one printed differentiation
step, preserves the source's final coefficient formula when restored.
This elementary calculation does not repair the geometric gap.

The trivial-bundle Martinelli specialization in the preprint still
assumes proper \(X\) and global regular components. On a proper
integral variety, \(H^0(X,\mathcal O_X)\) is a finite field extension
of \(K\), so each nonzero global function is a unit. A regular
positive-rank section of \(\mathcal O_X^{\oplus r}\) consequently
has empty zero scheme, component by component.
A nontrivial local affine formula requires additional current
machinery. The analytic Bochner--Martinelli identity of
\Cref{thm:cld-bochner-martinelli} belongs to its own stated CLD
framework.

\paragraph{Finite flat norms retain the entire fibre algebra.}
Now let \(K\) be an arbitrary complete non-Archimedean field and
let \(f:Y\to X\) be finite flat of constant degree \(d\).
For an analytic point \(x\), its finite fibre algebra decomposes as
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 A_x&=\prod_{y\mapsto x}A_y,\qquad
       \sum_{y\mapsto x}n_y=d,\\
 n_y&=\dim_{\mathcal H(x)}A_y
     =\operatorname{length}(A_y)
        [\mathcal H(y):\mathcal H(x)].
 \end{aligned}
\]
The length is intrinsic Artinian composition length; a compatible
coefficient-field embedding must not be silently presumed.
The fibre measure is \(\sum n_y\delta_y\), as in
\cite[Example~8.2]{BE21}. For an integral line bundle, its norm is
\[
 N_f(L)=\det f_*L\otimes(\det f_*\mathcal O_Y)^{-1}.
\]
The determinant/cocycle identification and base-change compatibility
are \cite[Lemmas~A.19--A.20]{BE21}. Rational line bundles are treated
after clearing denominators. The induced section norm satisfies \cite[Equation~(8.17)]{BE21}
\[
 \log|N_f(s)|_{N_f(\phi)}(x)
 =\sum_{y\mapsto x}n_y\log|s(y)|_\phi.
\]
Thus the additive weight is the negative logarithmic section norm.
For real functions the same weighted trace gives
\[
 N_f(f^*g)=dg,\qquad
 \|N_f(a)-N_f(b)\|_\infty\leq d\|a-b\|_\infty.
\]
These are multiplicity and uniform-estimate identities, distinct
from preservation of pshness.

For an elementary nilpotent diagnostic, take
\[
 K=\mathbf F_2((t)),\qquad
 A=K[z]/((z^2-t)^2),\qquad \epsilon=z^2-t.
\]
The algebra has rank four, length two and residue degree two;
\(\epsilon\ne0\) and \(\epsilon^2=0\). The determinant norm of
\(z\) is \(t^2\), while its residue valuation is \(1/2\).
The valuation identity is \(2=4\cdot(1/2)\), using both
multiplicity factors. This algebra is not geometrically reduced
and is not a counterexample to the psh norm theorem.

\paragraph{The exact psh norm input.}
\cite[Proposition~8.22]{BE21} assumes a finite flat morphism of
projective geometrically reduced \(K\)-schemes, an ample rational
line bundle on the source and a continuous global psh metric.
The norm metric is then continuous psh.
In the nontrivially valued case, the proof uses the uniform
nef-model approximation of \cite[Theorem~7.8]{BE21}, compatible
finite flat models, norm nefness and the degree-Lipschitz estimate.
The relevant trivialization has norm one on its reduction tube,
not on an arbitrary entire generic algebraic open.
Flattening and relative ampleness remain attributed classical inputs.
Theorem~7.8 requires ampleness and continuity; its merely semiample
extension is only an expectation in \cite[Remark~7.10]{BE21}.
The separate field-extension criterion
\cite[Theorem~7.31]{BE21} retains continuous, semiample and
geometrically reduced projective hypotheses and its earlier
supremum-norm/base-change input.

In the intended ample base-polarization application, type the
pullback norm as \(N_f(f^*L)\simeq L^{\otimes d}\).
The multiplicity-weighted average/maximum iteration has error
at most \((1-1/d)^kM_0\) for \(d>1\), because every fibre maximum
has positive integer weight at least one. Degree one is the
identity. The construction uses compact continuous global data
and the cited global psh norm input. It is not a descent theorem
for arbitrary local upper semicontinuous weak-psh functions.

These norm results do not supply the missing affine regularization
over nondiscrete \(\mathcal H(x)\), nor convert a coercive lower
bound into an upper majorant. Those are the separate interfaces of
\Cref{sec:appL-local-psh-growth-boundary}. Similarly, a model-Chow
Green current requires a comparison map before it can be used in
the analytic theory. Neither construction repairs the general
critical homogeneous Siu or envelope bridges, certifies LPWZ
density, or supplies a new independent-model receipt.

\section{Section norms, reduced fibres, and logarithmic integrability}
\label{sec:appL-supnorm-reduced-fibre-log-scope}
\index{supnorm!ground field extension}
\index{relative integral closure!nonfinite case}

The finite flat norm interface in
\cref{sec:appL-model-green-norm-scope} depends on several global
comparisons whose hypotheses should remain visible.
The following discussion uses the published Boucksom--Eriksson
article \cite{BE21}.  Its Section~6, reduced-fibre discussion and
logarithmic-integrability proof were read with their complete
selected text and matching rendered pages.
The three related source checkpoints cover fifty-three distinct
pages of the controlling 125-page PDF, including its cover;
they do not constitute a complete reading of the article.
Earlier affinoid, CLD and projective foundations remain attributed
inputs.

\paragraph{Scalar extension of section norms is asymptotic.}
Let \(K\) be complete and non-Archimedean, let \(X\) be projective
and geometrically reduced over \(K\), let \(L\) be a line bundle,
and let \(\phi\) be a continuous metric on \(L\).
For a complete extension \(F/K\), there are two norms on
\(H^0(X_F,mL_F)\): the supnorm of the pulled-back metric and
the scalar extension of the original supnorm.
For norms \(\alpha,\beta\) on a nonzero vector space, write
\[
 d_\infty(\alpha,\beta)
   =\sup_{v\ne0}\bigl|\log\alpha(v)-\log\beta(v)\bigr|;
\]
the distance on the zero vector space is zero.
The precise comparison is
\[
 d_\infty\!\left(\|\cdot\|_{m\phi_F},
                    (\|\cdot\|_{m\phi})_F\right)=o(m),
\]
with \(O(1)\) error when \(\phi\) is a model metric
\cite[Theorem~6.6]{BE21}.
Neither ampleness nor pshness is an assumption of this norm estimate.
Its constants concern fixed data; no uniformity over varying metrics
or extensions is asserted.

Equality on the original \(K\)-sections is weaker than equality on
