the entire extended section space.  The source proves only
\(\|\cdot\|_{\phi_F}\le(\|\cdot\|_\phi)_F\) there in general.
Its finite Galois, discretely valued criterion for exact equality
for every bounded metric is tameness
\cite[Lemmas~6.1--6.2]{BE21}.
The model comparison uses one scalar annihilating a coherent
special-fibre-supported quotient.  For a finite family of twists
on a common model, the bound is independent of their positive
tensor exponents; on \(r\)-fold products it can grow as \(C^r\)
\cite[Theorems~6.4--6.5]{BE21}.
The lattice scalar-extension statement is
\cite[Lemma~1.29(iv)]{BE21}; the later printed reference to
Lemma~1.28 abbreviates this additional step.

Uniform approximation of a continuous metric gives an error
at most \(C_\varepsilon+2m\varepsilon\).
Consequently the normalized limsup is at most \(2\varepsilon\),
and tends to zero as \(\varepsilon\) tends to zero.
The printed intermediate bound omits the factor two, without
changing the final conclusion.  The approximation constant
\(C_\varepsilon\) does not supply an unconditional rate.
These estimates apply to the global section-norm problem over
nondiscrete complete extensions as well.  They do not prove
the affine regularization required in
\cref{sec:appL-local-psh-growth-boundary}.

\paragraph{Relative integral closure can fail to be finite.}
The reduced-fibre comparison separates three conditions:
normality or relative integral closure of a total model,
reducedness of its special fibre, and finiteness of a chosen
relative integral closure.
Over a discrete valuation ring, even a regular total model can
have nonreduced special fibre.  For example,
\[
 R=\mathbf Q[[\pi]],\qquad B=\mathbf Q[[z]],\qquad \pi=z^2
\]
has special fibre \(\mathbf Q[z]/(z^2)\).
The dense-value-group converse in
\cite[Theorem~4.19]{BE21} is therefore essential.
The finite relative integral closure of
\cite[Theorem~4.20]{BE21} assumes that the nontrivially valued
field is discrete or algebraically closed; the latter case
also gives reduced special fibre.

The failure outside those hypotheses is already indicated in
\cite[Remark~4.21]{BE21}.  A concrete instance uses the completed
Gauss residue field \(H\) over \(\mathbf Q((\pi))\) with
\[
 v(\pi)=1,\qquad v(T)=\sqrt2,\qquad
 \Gamma_H=\mathbf Z+\sqrt2\,\mathbf Z.
\]
The group is dense and does not contain \(1/2\):
an equality \(m+n\sqrt2=1/2\) would imply
\((2m-1)^2=8n^2\), impossible modulo two.
Put \(L=H(z)\), where \(z^2=\pi\).
Then \([L:H]=2\) and \(v(z)=1/2\).
The finite free model
\[
 \mathscr A=H^\circ[z]/(z^2-\pi)
\]
has reduced generic fibre.  Its relative integral closure in \(L\)
is \(L^\circ\).  Indeed, the complete-field valuation has a unique
extension.  All conjugates of an element of nonnegative valuation
have nonnegative valuation, so its monic polynomial has integral
coefficients; an element of negative valuation cannot satisfy such
an equation.

The values of \(a\) and \(bz\), when both terms are nonzero, belong
to disjoint cosets of \(\Gamma_H\).  Hence
\[
 v(a+bz)=\min\{v(a),v(b)+\tfrac12\},\qquad
 L^\circ=H^\circ\oplus Iz,\qquad
 I=\{b\in H:v(b)>-\tfrac12\}.
\]
The ideal \(I\) has no least value, whereas every finitely
generated fractional ideal over a valuation ring is principal
and has a least value.  Thus \(L^\circ\) is not finite over
\(H^\circ\), or over \(\mathscr A\).
This is an explicit boundary of the cited relative-normalization
input.  The global norm proof passes to an algebraically closed
extension, where its required finite model construction is
available.  The example does not contradict that proof.

\paragraph{A regularized maximum is needed at a zero.}
The global logarithmic-integrability statement concerns a
projective scheme of dimension \(n\), its fundamental \(n\)-cycle,
continuous psh-regularizable metrics \(\phi_1,\ldots,\phi_n\)
and a regular section \(s\) of a line bundle \(L\).
For any continuous metric \(\phi\) on \(L\),
\(\log|s|_\phi\) is integrable against
\[
 dd^c\phi_1\wedge\cdots\wedge dd^c\phi_n\wedge\delta_X,
\]
and its integral depends continuously on the \(\phi_i\) in the
uniform topology \cite[Theorem~8.12]{BE21}.
The regular-section hypothesis cannot be replaced by a nonzero
global section: on two disjoint projective lines the section
\((0,X_1)\) vanishes identically on a component carrying positive
curvature mass.

The printed non-Archimedean construction in
\cite[Lemma~8.13]{BE21} uses ordinary log-of-sum-of-squares
weights and calls them smooth.  Near a rational zero, the weight
\(\frac12\log(1+|T|^2)\) is not CLD-smooth.
Every CLD-smooth function there depends on finitely many logarithms
of analytic units, whose absolute values are constant on a
sufficiently small neighbourhood.  The displayed weight is zero
at \(T=0\) and positive at \(T=\pi^k\) for every \(k\).
The Archimedean construction has no such defect.
The non-Archimedean proof can instead use the regularized-maximum
input in \cite[Example~7.12 and Theorem~7.14]{BE21}, as follows.

For \(n=0\), a regular section is a unit on each local Artinian
factor, and its logarithmic norm is finite on the finite analytic
space.  The following argument concerns \(n\ge1\).
Choose very ample \(A'\) and \(A=L+A'\), and Fubini--Study
metrics \(\psi'\) on \(A'\) and \(\theta\) on \(A\).
The metrics
\[
 \psi_j=\max\{\log|s|+\psi',\,\theta-j\}
\]
on \(A\) are continuous Fubini--Study metrics: use the products
of \(s\) with the chosen \(A'\) generators and the \(A\)
generators with shifted weights.
They are psh-regularizable and decrease to
\(\log|s|+\psi'\).
Choose the reference metric
\(\phi_{\rm ref}=\theta-\psi'\).  Then
\[
 f_j=\psi_j-\theta\ \searrow\
       \log|s|_{\phi_{\rm ref}}.
\]
Both metrics on \(A\) in this difference are explicitly
psh-regularizable.  Merely choosing an arbitrary smooth dpsh
reference would not establish that premise.

The global Chern--Levine--Nirenberg estimate in
\cite[Lemma~8.6]{BE21} extends from smooth semipositive metrics
by uniform approximation.  Its expansion of
\(dd^c(\phi_0-\phi'_0)\) needs a minus sign between the two
integrals; the printed plus sign does not alter the final
absolute-value estimate obtained from their two positive masses.
The estimate compares the \(f_j\) integrals for two tuples with
a constant independent of \(j\).
Fix one smooth semipositive tuple from the assumed
regularizations.  The cited local smooth logarithmic-integrability
input, and a bounded change of reference metric, give a finite
integral for this tuple.
Since the \(f_j\) decrease and are bounded above, monotone
convergence and that finite anchor give finite integrals for
every tuple and the asserted uniform continuity.
This uses finite inequalities throughout, without subtraction
of two infinite values.
Changing to any continuous metric adds a bounded continuous
function, whose integral is continuous by
\cite[Theorem~8.4]{BE21}.

This is an explanatory reformulation of the cited theorem with
its existing global inputs.  The associated continuous dpsh
Poincar\'e--Lelong formula and Deligne-pairing restriction retain
the regular section, uniform approximation and minus-log
conventions.  Weak convergence alone does not control the
unbounded logarithm.
Neither this argument nor the norm estimate supplies arbitrary
local weak-psh descent, affine lower-growth regularization,
a model-Chow-to-CLD comparison, or the general critical
homogeneous Siu and envelope input.  The warning about BJ22
Theorem~6.21 and the open status ledger remain unchanged.

\section{Graded norm normalization and upper semicontinuous FS limits}
\label{sec:appL-graded-FS-normalization-scope}
\index{graded norm!spectral normalization}
\index{Fubini--Study metric!upper semicontinuous regularization}

Reboulet's \cite{Reb20AsymptoticFSV1} studies bounded submultiplicative
graded norms on the section ring of an ample line bundle over a
projective geometrically integral variety and a complete
non-Archimedean field. The controlling source is the forty-three-page
arXiv v1 of 24~April~2020. The following normalization and worked
FS calculation are direct consequences of the definitions.
The general correspondence is retained with its separate hypotheses
and proof dependencies.

\paragraph{Signed means, absolute means and degree.}
For a nonzero \(N_m\)-dimensional section space and two norms
\(\zeta_m,\eta_m\), let \(\lambda_{m,i}\) be the logarithmic
ratios of \(\eta_m\) to \(\zeta_m\). Their rescaled spectral
probability measure is
\[
 \sigma_m=\frac1{N_m}\sum_{i=1}^{N_m}
                  \delta_{\lambda_{m,i}/m}.
\]
Its signed first moment is the normalized relative volume divided
by \(m\); its absolute first moment is \(d_1(\zeta_m,\eta_m)/m\).
These are different quantities. The finite-dimensional displays
in \cite[Definition~1.4.4]{Reb20AsymptoticFSV1} omit absolute values
in two places; the defining absolute-moment formula controls the
distance. A spectral probability always includes division by
\(N_m\). For an ample line bundle on an \(n\)-dimensional variety,
\(N_m\sim\operatorname{vol}(L)m^n/n!\); this converts the
determinant normalization of \cite{BE21} to the normalized mean.

If \(R(X,rL)\) is assigned degree \(k\), that degree corresponds
to degree \(rk\) of \(R(X,L)\). Division by \(k\) multiplies
the normalized logarithmic weights by \(r\) compared with
division by \(rk\). Consequently a volume computed after
regrading must be divided by \(r\) before comparison with the
original degree convention. On a point, the multiplicative norm
\(\zeta_m=e^{-cm}\) has signed volume \(c\) against the trivial
norm; its restriction, regraded as a norm on \(rL\), has volume
\(rc\). This elementary check is indispensable when reading
\cite[Proposition~3.3.5 and Theorem~4.5.4]{Reb20AsymptoticFSV1},
whose later approximation proof mixes the two degree conventions.

\paragraph{A section direction invisible to asymptotic \(d_1\).}
Let \(K\) be any complete non-Archimedean field, set
\(X=\mathbf P^1_K\), and take \(L=\mathcal O(1)\).
In degree \(m\ge1\), write
\(s=\sum_{i=0}^m a_iX_0^{m-i}X_1^i\), and define
\[
 \eta_m(s)=\max_{0\le i\le m}|a_i|,
 \qquad
 \zeta_m(s)=\max\{2^m|a_0|,\,|a_1|,\ldots,|a_m|\}.
\]
The degree-zero norm is the ground absolute value.
Both are diagonal norms. The first is the Gauss norm.
For a product of degree-\(m\) and degree-\(k\) sections, its
Gauss norm is at most the product of their Gauss norms, and its
penalized constant coefficient satisfies
\[
 2^{m+k}|a_0b_0|=(2^m|a_0|)(2^k|b_0|)
                  \le\zeta_m(s)\zeta_k(t).
\]
These two bounds prove submultiplicativity of \(\zeta_\bullet\).
Moreover \(\eta_m\le\zeta_m\le2^m\eta_m\), so it is bounded
in the explicit logarithmic \(O(m)\) sense.

Put \(a=\log2\). Simultaneous diagonalization gives
\[
 \sigma_m=\frac{\delta_{-a}+m\delta_0}{m+1},
 \qquad
 \frac1m d_1(\zeta_m,\eta_m)=\frac{a}{m+1}\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad
 \frac1m d_\infty(\zeta_m,\eta_m)=a.
\]
The weak spectral limit is \(\delta_0\), although the normalized
operator distance stays positive. This is a worked instance of
the distinction in \cite[Remark~3.2.2]{Reb20AsymptoticFSV1}; it
does not use or prove a general quotient correspondence.

\paragraph{The raw FS limit and its regularization.}
On the chart \(X_0\ne0\), put \(T=X_1/X_0\) and
\(\rho=|T(x)|\). Use weights in the frame \(X_0\), rather
than potentials relative to an unstated reference. The standard
FS weight is \(\phi_0=\log\max\{1,\rho\}\), and the
degree-\(m\) FS weight associated to \(\zeta_m\) is
\[
 \phi_m=\frac1m\log\max\{2^{-m},\rho,\ldots,\rho^m\}
 =\begin{cases}
    \max\{-a,\,m^{-1}\log\rho\},&0\le\rho\le1,\\
    \log\rho,&\rho\ge1.
   \end{cases}
\]
Here \(\log0=-\infty\); the maximum at \(\rho=0\) is finite.
The homogeneous expression defines a continuous global FS metric
in every degree, and \(\phi_m\) increases. Its raw pointwise
limit \(\phi\) equals \(\phi_0\) away from the rational point
\(T=0\), but equals \(-a\) at that point. Thus
\(-a\le\phi-\phi_0\le0\).
Gauss points of positive radii tending to zero converge to \(T=0\)
and have limiting weight zero. Hence \(\phi\) is not upper
semicontinuous, and \(\phi^*=\phi_0\).

For this bounded obstacle, define \(Q(\phi)\) as the supremum
of continuous global psh metrics below \(\phi\).
Every \(\phi_m\) is such a minorant, and their supremum is
\(\phi\); the obstacle inequality gives the reverse bound.
Therefore
\[
             Q(\phi)=\phi,\qquad Q(\phi)^*=\phi_0.
\]
No continuity-of-envelopes assumption is needed for this explicit
calculation. It explains why an asymptotic FS operator defined
with upper semicontinuous regularization must not be identified
with the unregularized \(Q\) for every bounded obstacle.
Over an algebraically closed trivially valued field, shifting
and scaling the weights identifies this calculation with
\cite[Example~2.21]{BJKS1}. That primary example separates
homogeneity of a graded norm from upper semicontinuity of its
raw FS function.
The bounded-obstacle convergence in
\cite[Proposition~7.26]{BE21} and the definition in
\cite[Definition~5.2.4]{Reb20AsymptoticFSV1} retain precisely this
distinction.

\paragraph{Scope of the general comparison.}
\cite[Theorem~5.4.1]{Reb20AsymptoticFSV1} explicitly assumes
continuity of envelopes and an ample polarization. Its v1 proof
also needs correction or applicable external inputs at several
interfaces. Diagonal norm density is distinct from lattice norm
density, which requires a dense value group. Semiample alone
does not imply a big Okounkov body or \(X=\operatorname{Proj}R(X,L)\).
Submultiplicativity gives superadditivity before division by the
degree; its FS values need not be decreasing. Finally, a valuation
\(c\operatorname{ord}_E\) from a horizontal divisor on a
\(K\)-birational model is trivial on \(K^*\), so over a
nontrivially valued field it is not a point of the prescribed
\(X^{\An}\). The determination argument requires a correctly
typed analytic valuation family and its actual uniqueness input.
These qualifications prevent using that v1 proof alone as a
general envelope or local-to-global comparison theorem.
The elementary calculations above introduce no new general result;
the BJ22 Theorem~6.21 proof warning and the open-status ledger
remain in force.

\section{Norm rays, maximal apartments and volume limits}
\label{sec:appL-norm-ray-volume-interfaces}
\index{apartment!maximal system}
\index{relative volume!probability normalization}

\Cref{sec:rf-norm-envelope-rays} gives the elementary
finite-dimensional comparison. The controlling published
edition is \cite{Finski25SubmultiplicativePublished}, DOI
10.1112/plms.70077; all forty-seven physical pages of its
published author copy were read. Lemma~2.6 is the norm comparison.
Corollary~3.5 assumes bounded submultiplicative graded norms
on an ample complex section ring and uses regularized FS
metrics for finite~\(p\). Its infinity extension additionally
requires continuous raw FS limits. Section~4 supplies the
tensor argument, including the fixed-variable Parseval step
explained in \Cref{sec:rf-norm-envelope-rays}. The original
holomorphic extension and Darvas quantization inputs remain
separate cited dependencies.

\paragraph{The canonical FS representative needs its own input.}
Over an algebraically closed trivially valued field, let
\(X\) be projective, reduced and irreducible, and \(L\) an
ample \(\mathbb Q\)-line bundle. For a linearly bounded
multiplicative additive norm \(\chi\) on its section ring,
\cite[Definition~2.13]{BJKS1} defines raw
\(\operatorname{FS}(\chi)\) as a bounded lower semicontinuous
supremum of finite-degree FS functions. This need not be psh;
\cite[Example~2.21]{BJKS1} gives the point-supported downward
jump calculated in \Cref{sec:appL-graded-FS-normalization-scope}.
Write \(\operatorname{FS}^*(\chi)\) for its upper
semicontinuous regularization. The precise statement of
\cite[Lemma~6.17(iii)]{BJKS1} assumes that this regularization
is \(L\)-psh before concluding that the canonical
approximants converge strongly in \(\mathcal E^1\).
The full envelope property supplies that membership.
The weaker envelope property gives equality on linear-growth
valuations and equality of extended energies; it does not
by itself give the full psh assertion.

Accordingly the raw supremum in
\cite[equation~(6.15)]{Finski25SubmultiplicativePublished}
must be separated from the canonical psh extension used in
its Theorem~6.6. This distinction corrects the literal
membership interface without rejecting the intended maximal-ray
statement. The proof of the BJKS1 lemma imports the
non-negligibility and finite-energy inputs from \cite{BJ22}
and its separate addendum. Those inputs retain their own
source obligations; this citation chain supplies no repair
of the reported BJ22 Theorem~6.21 Siu proof gap.
\index{Fubini--Study operator!upper regularization}

\paragraph{Functions must be defined at finite spectral values.}
The nineteen-page arXiv:2311.13451v2 of 29~October~2025,
\cite{RWN25FlatsArxivV2}, was read in full. Its published IMRN
edition is a different source edition and is not claimed read.
The revised construction explicitly extends its bounded
decreasing convex functions beyond the limiting DH support
before evaluating them on finite-degree weights. If the support
begins at \(a\) and \(f'_+(a)\) is finite, the extension
below \(a\) is
\[
          \widetilde f(x)=f(a)+f'_+(a)(x-a),\qquad x<a.
\]
It is evaluated on a common bounded interval containing the
finite spectra. A limiting support is insufficient for this
finite operation: the empirical probabilities
\[
               \frac{\delta_{-1}+m\delta_0}{m+1}
\]
have limiting support \(\{0\}\), but every finite measure
still evaluates the function at \(-1\).
Two bounded extensions agreeing at zero differ in their
finite-\(p\) costs by at most a fixed constant times
\((m+1)^{-1/p}\); their maximal difference at the outlier
need not vanish. This elementary example explains the
extension and the endpoint distinction without proving a
general flat-construction theorem.

The revised source's older Finski citation numbers must be
mapped to the controlling edition. The substantive interfaces
in the published copy are Lemma~2.6 and Corollary~3.5 as above.
The complex envelope is generally not Hermitian, and its
logarithmic-rank comparison replaces any claimed finite exact
identity. Squared metric potentials, genuine norm lengths,
the degree power in \(d_p^p/m^p\), and probability rather
than unnormalized DH mass must be calibrated together.
Completion in \(\mathcal E^p\) also does not turn individually
bounded approximants into a commonly bounded family; any
bounded-limit assertion needs its own common estimate.

\paragraph{The apartment system is part of the hypothesis.}
The forty-one-page author copy of \cite{Parreau00AffineAuthor}
was read on physical pages~21--26 for the maximal-system
argument. Proposition~2.17 constructs the unique maximal
apartment system containing a given one. Proposition~2.18
then equates maximality with containment of every geodesic
ray and line in an apartment. Its proof takes compatible
apartments on successively larger bounded regions and uses
closure under their inductive limits.
The entire Euclidean-flat containment in Proposition~2.25
is stated after maximality is explicitly assumed.

Thus an application to a chosen basis-norm atlas needs an
identification with the maximal system, or a separate direct
containment argument. Finding apartments for finite segments
alone does not complete that step. Nor does a general Euclidean
CAT(0) argument apply verbatim to a non-strictly-convex
\(L^1\) metric. A nonessential building also retains its
scalar Euclidean factor. Already in rank one, all positive
Hermitian norms form a logarithmic real line, whereas the
determinant-normalized \(\mathrm{SL}_1/\mathrm{SU}_1\)
quotient is a point.

\paragraph{Relative volume is a signed first-moment statement.}
Published \cite[Section~9.1]{BE21} assumes a complete valued
field and geometrically reduced projective \(X\); its
Theorem~9.5 adds geometric integrality. A graded norm is
submultiplicative and logarithmically \(O(m)\)-close to the
sup norms of a fixed bounded metric. The theorem permits
arbitrary \(L\). For nonbig \(L\), the section dimension
is \(o(m^n)\) and its relative-volume limit is zero.
For big \(L\), Lemma~9.4 first permits a complete
algebraically closed extension, with total volume error
\(o(m^{n+1})\), before a regular rational point and
one-dimensional Okounkov leaves are chosen.
The two negative leaf-log functions are separately
superadditive; their difference need not be.

The normalization in Definition~9.6 is
\[
 \operatorname{vol}_{\mathrm{BE}}(\zeta_\bullet,\eta_\bullet)
   =\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{n!}{m^{n+1}}
                      \operatorname{vol}(\zeta_m,\eta_m).
\]
A probability spectral mean instead divides the finite
determinant volume by \(mN_m\). For an ample line bundle,
the conversion factor tends to \(\operatorname{vol}(L)\).
On \(\mathbf P^1\) with \(\mathcal O(2)\), a constant
logarithmic slope \(c\) gives BE volume \(2c\) and
probability mean \(c\). A signed first-moment limit alone
does not establish all absolute moments or a spectral law.

Physical pages~87--94 of the published BE21 copy were read
for this selection. The final local coefficient argument
needs a nontrivially valued extension or an analytic
Gauss-polydisc interpretation when \(K\) is trivially valued:
a classical disc of radius \(r<1\) then contains only zero,
so the classical supremum of \(z\) is zero.
The component proof of Lemma~9.9 similarly needs to retain
lower-dimensional components or their restriction kernel
on a nonpure scheme. For \(\mathbf P^1\) disjoint union
a point, restriction to the top-dimensional component has
a one-dimensional kernel. Its contribution is lower order,
so this diagnoses the displayed exact sequence rather than
refuting the stated leading-order formula.
